## Micah - Introduction (id: en_tn_MIC_hp4d)

Introduction to Micah
=====================

Part 1: General Introduction
----------------------------

### Outline of Micah

1. Yahweh announces judgment on his people and their leaders (1–3\)
2. Yahweh promises future restoration and righteous rule (4–5\)
3. Yahweh calls his people to repentance and offers hope (6–7\)

### What is the Book of Micah about?

This book contains many of Micah’s prophecies. Micah repeated many similar prophecies as found in Isaiah. Micah warned the kingdoms of Israel and Judah that the Assyrians would soon attack them. Micah condemned those who acted unjustly and immorally. He spoke against those who did not work for a living. He also spoke against the false prophets in Israel and Judah. These false prophets were telling the people that Yahweh would never let a foreign kingdom harm or conquer them.

Micah called the people to please Yahweh by living righteous and holy lives. However, the sinful way they lived and the unjust way they treated their fellow countrymen proved that they no longer wanted to obey and honor Yahweh.

### How should the title of this book be translated?

Translators may decide to translate the traditional title “The Book of Micah” or just “Micah” in a way that is clearer to the readers. They may decide to call it “The Sayings of Micah.”

### Who wrote Micah?

The prophet Micah wrote this book. Micah lived at the same time as Isaiah and Hosea. This was sometime between 750 and 700 B.C., during the reigns of Jotham, Ahaz, and Hezekiah, kings of Judah. He began prophesying when he lived in the northern kingdom of Israel. Then he moved to the kingdom of Judah.

Part 2: Important Religious and Cultural Concepts
-------------------------------------------------

### Who is the deliverer Micah mentions?

Micah frequently mentioned a king who would deliver the people. It is clear that Micah meant the Messiah, the descendant of David who would rule forever, though he never referred to him as the Messiah.

### Were the Israelites required to offer sacrifices or live holy lives to please Yahweh?

Many of the Old Testament writers were concerned with offering sacrifices according to the law of Moses. However, Micah explained to the people that Yahweh was pleased only when they offered these sacrifices in faith. Without faith, the sacrifices had no meaning.

Part 3: Important Translation Issues
------------------------------------

### What does a translator need to know before translating Micah?

Micah did not often explain how his prophecies were historically situated. This may result in the translator failing to understand implicit information unless they understand Micah’s time. It is therefore suggested that translators understand well the history of the kingdoms of Israel and Judah.

### How do I translate passages where the speaker or addressee is not mentioned explicitly?

Micah did not usually say who was speaking or who was being addressed. Reading the verses before and after the quote will often help the reader to understand who is speaking or being addressed. It is acceptable to make this information explicit so the reader can more easily understand it.

### “let” and “may”

The ULT uses the English helping verbs “let” and “may” to show that a speaker is expressing a desire that something happen or that someone do something. It uses the verb “let” for more assertive expressions, and it uses the verb “may” for more deferential expressions. For example, when David says in Psalms 69:34 “Let the heavens and the earth praise him,” he means “I want the heavens and the earth to praise him.” Languages that do not have a volitional form might say “The heavens and earth ought to praise him,” or they might use an imperative, “Praise him, heavens and earth!” When Micah says in 5:9 “May your hand rise over your adversaries,” he is being more deferential, even though his wish is urgent, because he is addressing Yahweh, a superior. Languages that do not have a volitional form might say “Yahweh, I desire that your hand rise over your adversaries,” or they might use an imperative with an indication that it is a polite request, “Yahweh, please defeat your adversaries.” Translate this volitional form in the most natural way in your language to indicate that the speaker is expressing what he wants someone to do or what he wishes someone would do.

### How should the text of Micah be formatted?

The ULT uses a poetic language style like the Hebrew text. Many languages can do this but some languages will not be able to do this. The ULT also sets apart the poetic portions of the text by setting the poetic lines farther to the right on the page than the rest of the text. With the exception of the first verse, the entire book is indented because the prophecies were written as a series of poems.

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 0:0


## Micah - Chapter 1 Introduction (id: en_tn_MIC_e47z)

Micah 1 Chapter Introduction
============================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

Micah is written mainly in a poetic form. Most of the prophets wrote to either the Northern Kingdom or the Southern Kingdom. Micah wrote to both of them at times without clear distinction, as he did in this chapter.

1. Yahweh comes to judge the nations (1–5\)
2. Samaria is destroyed because of its sin (6–7\)
3. Micah mourns the coming judgment on Judah (8–16\)

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### Idolatry as prostitution

Samaria’s idols are called a prostitute’s wages, portraying idolatry as unfaithfulness to Yahweh.

Translation Issues in This Chapter
----------------------------------

### City names

Several times in this chapter, the names of the cities are used to describe their destruction. This sounds unusual in English because of its construction in Hebrew. It contains several “plays on words.” “Lachish will be lashed” is an example of this type of construction. This kind of poetic element rarely carries into other languages but including the information in a footnote may be helpful.

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 1:0


## Micah 1:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_ujde)

דְּבַר־יְהוָ֣ה ׀ אֲשֶׁ֣ר הָיָ֗ה אֶל־מִיכָה֙ הַ⁠מֹּ֣רַשְׁתִּ֔י בִּ⁠ימֵ֥י יוֹתָ֛ם אָחָ֥ז יְחִזְקִיָּ֖ה מַלְכֵ֣י יְהוּדָ֑ה אֲשֶׁר־חָזָ֥ה עַל־שֹׁמְר֖וֹן וִ⁠ירֽוּשָׁלִָֽם

**"The word of Yahweh that was to Micah the Morashtite in the days of Jotham, Ahaz, {and} Hezekiah, kings of Judah, which he saw concerning Samaria and Jerusalem"**

Micah is speaking of himself in the third person as **Micah** and **he** to introduce this book.

See: First, Second or Third Person

## Micah 1:2 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_utyh)

הַקְשִׁ֖יבִי אֶ֣רֶץ

**"pay attention, earth"**

Micah is speaking to the **earth**, which he knows cannot hear him. He is doing this to communicate to the people listening to him that he feels very strongly about the judgment that Yahweh is about to bring. If it would be helpful in your language, you could translate these words as if he were speaking directly to the people about the earth. Alternate translation: \[everyone on earth should pay attention]

See: Apostrophe

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 1:2
* **Associated Articles:** Apostrophe (ID: `33259@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

אֶ֣רֶץ וּ⁠מְלֹאָ֑⁠הּ

**"earth, and its fullness"**

Here, **its fullness** represents everything and everyone that fills the earth. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[earth, and everything and everyone in it]

וְ⁠דָרַ֖ךְ עַל־במותי אָֽרֶץ

**"and tread on the high places of the earth"**

## Micah 1:4 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_feam)

וְ⁠נָמַ֤סּוּ הֶֽ⁠הָרִים֙ תַּחְתָּ֔י⁠ו

**"And the mountains will melt under him"**

Micah is speaking of the effect of Yahweh’s powerful presence as if the solid **mountains will melt under him**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[And it will be like the mountains will soften and flow out from beneath him]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 1:4
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)


## Micah 1:4 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_ifib)

וְ⁠הָ⁠עֲמָקִ֖ים יִתְבַּקָּ֑עוּ כַּ⁠דּוֹנַג֙ מִ⁠פְּנֵ֣י הָ⁠אֵ֔שׁ כְּ⁠מַ֖יִם מֻגָּרִ֥ים בְּ⁠מוֹרָֽד

**"and the valleys will split open, like wax from the face of the fire, like waters poured down a steep place"**

If it would be more natural in your language, you may want to put the comparisons of the wax and the water with the information about the mountains melting as modeled in the UST.

See: Information Structure

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 1:4
* **Associated Articles:** Information Structure (ID: `33286@UWTranslationManual`)


## Micah 1:4 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_hi5e)

כַּ⁠דּוֹנַג֙ מִ⁠פְּנֵ֣י הָ⁠אֵ֔שׁ

**"like wax from the face of the fire"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **wax** melts when it is near a **fire**, so it will be as if the mountains will melt away because of the power of Yahweh’s presence. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[just as wax melts when it is near a fire]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 1:4
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationManual`)


## Micah 1:4 (#4) (id: en_tn_MIC_xsem)

מִ⁠פְּנֵ֣י הָ⁠אֵ֔שׁ

**"from the face of the fire"**

Micah is using **from the face of** as a common expression of the culture to mean “because of” or “on account of.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[melts because of the heat of the fire]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 1:4
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationManual`)


## Micah 1:4 (#5) (id: en_tn_MIC_n7fg)

כְּ⁠מַ֖יִם מֻגָּרִ֥ים בְּ⁠מוֹרָֽד

**"like waters poured down a steep place"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **waters** rush swiftly and unstoppably down a **steep place**, so the will the mountains quickly melt before the powerful presence of Yahweh. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[as swiftly and unstoppably as waters rushing down a steep place]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 1:4
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationManual`)


## Micah 1:4 (#6) (id: en_tn_MIC_orkz)

כְּ⁠מַ֖יִם מֻגָּרִ֥ים בְּ⁠מוֹרָֽד

**"like waters poured down a steep place"**

The expression **poured** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[like waters that pour down from a steep place]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 1:4
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 1:5 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_bapi)

בֵּ֣ית יִשְׂרָאֵ֑ל

**"the house of Israel"**

Here, **house** means all the people descended from a particular person. It envisions them as if they were one household living together. So **the house of Israel** figuratively means all the people descended from the patriarch Jacob, who was also known as Israel. Alternate translation: \[the Israelites]

See: Metaphor

**"Who {is} the transgression of Jacob? {Is} it not Samaria"**

Micah is using the question form for emphasis. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[Samaria is the cause of Jacob’s transgression!]

## Micah 1:8 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_f2ma)

אֶסְפְּדָ֣ה וְ⁠אֵילִ֔ילָה

**"I shall lament and wail"**

The terms **lament** and **wail** mean similar things. Micah is using the two terms together for emphasis. If it would be clearer for your readers, you could express the emphasis with a single phrase. Alternate translation: \[I shall greatly mourn]

See: Doublet

## Micah 1:8 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_p4km)

אֵילְכָ֥ה שילל וְ⁠עָר֑וֹם

**"I shall go barefoot and naked"**

Going about without sandals or clothing was a symbolic action by which Micah showed his deep grief and mourning over the coming disaster. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could explain the significance of this action in the text of your translation or in a footnote. Alternate translation: \[I shall go barefoot and naked to show my grief]

See: Symbolic Action

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 1:8
* **Associated Articles:** Symbolic Action (ID: `33402@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Ostrich (ID: `fauna:Ostrich`); Owl (ID: `fauna:Owl`); Jackal (ID: `fauna:Jackal`)

## Micah 1:8 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_wj97)

אֶעֱשֶׂ֤ה מִסְפֵּד֙ כַּ⁠תַּנִּ֔ים

**"I shall make a lamentation like the jackals"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **the jackals** make loud, wailing cries, so Micah’s **lamentation** will be loud and mournful. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[I shall make a lamentation as loud and mournful as the cries of the jackals]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 1:8
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Ostrich (ID: `fauna:Ostrich`); Owl (ID: `fauna:Owl`); Jackal (ID: `fauna:Jackal`)

מִסְפֵּד֙

**"a lamentation"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **lamentation**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[mournful wailing]

## Micah 1:8 (#5) (id: en_tn_MIC_b4si)

וְ⁠אֵ֖בֶל כִּ⁠בְנ֥וֹת יַעֲנָֽה

**"and mourning like the daughters of an owl"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **the daughters of an owl** make mournful, crying sounds, so Micah’s **mourning** will sound. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[and mourning that sounds like the crying of young owls]

## Micah 1:8 (#6) (id: en_tn_MIC_z0dl)

וְ⁠אֵ֖בֶל כִּ⁠בְנ֥וֹת יַעֲנָֽה

**"and mourning like the daughters of an owl"**

Micah is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[and I shall make mourning like the daughters of an owl]

אֲנוּשָׁ֖ה מַכּוֹתֶ֑י⁠הָ

Micah is speaking of the destruction that has struck the city as if it were **wounds** that cannot be healed. שַׁ֥עַר עַמִּ֖⁠י

**"the gate of my people"**

Here, **gate** represents the city of Jerusalem itself, since the gate was the main entrance to the city and the place where it was defended. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[the city of my people]

## Micah 1:10 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_ui2z)

בָּכ֖וֹ אַל־תִּבְכּ֑וּ

**"weeping, do not weep"**

Micah is repeating forms of the verb **weep** in order to intensify his expression of the idea that it conveys. If your language can repeat words for intensification, it would be appropriate to do that here in your translation. If not, your language may have another way of expressing the intensification. Alternate translation: \[certainly you should not weep]

## Micah 1:11 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_e9y9)

שָׁפִ֖יר עֶרְיָה־בֹ֑שֶׁת לֹ֤א יָֽצְאָה֙ יוֹשֶׁ֣בֶת צַֽאֲנָ֔ן מִסְפַּד֙ בֵּ֣ית הָאֵ֔צֶל יִקַּ֥ח מִ⁠כֶּ֖ם

**"Shaphir, {in} nakedness of shame. The dweller of Zaanan has not gone out; the mourning of Beth Ezel will take from you"**

The name of the town **Shaphir** means “pleasant” or “beautiful,” the opposite of **nakedness** and **shame**. The name of the town **Zaanan** sounds like the verb “go out.” The name of the town **Beth Ezel** may mean “house of taking away.” If it would be helpful to your readers, you could put this information in a footnote.

רֵאשִׁ֨ית חַטָּ֥את הִיא֙

**"She {was} the beginning of sin"**

Even though Micah has addressed the people of Lachish directly as **dweller of Lachish**, here he speaks of them in the third person as **She** and then switches back to addressing them as **you**. לָ⁠כֵן֙

**"Therefore"**

The word translated as **Therefore** indicates that what follows is a result of what came before. Use a connector in your language that makes it clear that what follows is a result of what came before. Alternate translation: \[As a result,]

* **Associated Articles:** Connect — Reason-and-Result Relationship (ID: `33331@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 1:14 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_aquy)

תִּתְּנִ֣י שִׁלּוּחִ֔ים עַ֖ל מוֹרֶ֣שֶׁת גַּ֑ת

**"you will give dowry gifts to Moresheth Gath"**

The town **Moresheth Gath** is Micah’s hometown. The part of the name **Moresheth** sounds like the word for “betrothed.” The idea is that, just as a father gave a gift of money (a dowry) along with his daughter to the man she married, the leaders of Jerusalem will have to give tribute to Assyria as they also give them their towns, such as Moresheth Gath.

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

עַד־עֲדֻּלָּ֥ם יָב֖וֹא כְּב֥וֹד יִשְׂרָאֵֽל

**"The glory of Israel will come to Adullam"**

By **The glory of Israel will come to Adullam**, Micah means that the honored leaders of Israel will go into hiding there, just as David once hid in the caves near Adullam to escape his enemies (described in 1 Samuel 22:1\). You could indicate this explicitly in your translation if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[Israel’s honored leaders will flee to hide in the caves near Adullam, as David once did]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 1:15
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationManual`)

קָרְחִ֣י וָ⁠גֹ֔זִּי

**"Make yourself bald and shave"**

The terms translated as **Make yourself bald** and **shave** mean similar things. Micah is using the two terms together for emphasis. If it would be clearer for your readers, you could express the emphasis with a single phrase. Alternate translation: \[Shave your head completely bald]

קָרְחִ֣י וָ⁠גֹ֔זִּי

**"Make yourself bald and shave"**

Shaving the head completely bald was a symbolic action by which the people would show their deep grief and mourning. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could explain the significance of this action in the text of your translation or in a footnote. Alternate translation: \[Make yourself bald and shave to show your grief]

Here the masculine term **sons** has a generic sense that includes all children whether male or female. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could use language in your translation that is clearly inclusive of both men and women as modeled in the UST.

## Micah - Chapter 2 Introduction (id: en_tn_MIC_jy29)

Micah 2 Chapter Introduction
============================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

This chapter continues using poetic forms in communicating that people who take advantage of others are guilty of sin and injustice.

1. Yahweh condemns those who oppress others (1–5\)
2. Yahweh rebukes those who reject his word (6–11\)
3. Yahweh promises to gather and restore his people (12–13\)

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### Land as inheritance

A family’s fields were an inheritance from Yahweh meant to stay in the family, so seizing them (verses 2, 4\) violated covenant order and invited his judgment.

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 2:0


## Micah 2:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_ydsk)

ה֧וֹי חֹֽשְׁבֵי

**"Woe to the devisers of"**

Micah is using the word **Woe** to express a strong feeling of warning about the coming judgment on the people he is describing. There may be an equivalent word or phrase in your language that you can use in your translation to convey this feeling. Alternate translation: \[Great disaster will come upon the devisers of]

## Micah 2:5 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_vkqa)

מַשְׁלִ֥יךְ חֶ֖בֶל בְּ⁠גוֹרָ֑ל

**"one casting a measuring line by lot"**

Micah assumes that readers will know that **casting a measuring line by lot** refers to the way the tribes received the land in Numbers 26:55\. Since Yahweh had promised Abraham the land as an eternal inheritance, Micah knew they would some day return from exile and the land would once again be divided up, but those who Yahweh is judging here will not be a part of that. You could say that explicitly as in the UST if that would be helpful to your readers.

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 2:5
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationManual`)

Micah is using the possessive form to mean the **assembly** that belongs to **Yahweh,** that is, when his people gather once again to divide up the land after returning from exile. If a speaker of your language would not use the possessive form for this, you could express this idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[among Yahweh’s people]

הֶ⁠אָמ֣וּר

**"Should it be said"**

The expression **Should it be said** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[Should anyone say]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 2:7
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Prophets (ID: `group:GenericProphets`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Micah 2:7 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_pd9q)

בֵּֽית־יַעֲקֹ֗ב

**"house of Jacob"**

Here, **house** means all the people descended from a particular person. It envisions them as if they were one household living together. So **the house of Jacob** figuratively means all the people descended from the patriarch Jacob, who was also known as Israel. Alternate translation: \[you Israelites]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 2:7
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Prophets (ID: `group:GenericProphets`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Micah 2:7 (#4) (id: en_tn_MIC_jnhn)

הֲ⁠קָצַר֙ ר֣וּחַ יְהוָ֔ה

**"Is the Spirit of Yahweh short"**

Here Micah is speaking of Yahweh being impatient as if his Spirit could be **short**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[Is Yahweh impatient]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 2:7
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Prophets (ID: `group:GenericProphets`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Micah 2:7 (#5) (id: en_tn_MIC_bled)

הֲ⁠קָצַר֙ ר֣וּחַ יְהוָ֔ה

**"Is the Spirit of Yahweh short"**

Micah is using the question form to affirm that the Spirit of Yahweh is not impatient. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[The Spirit of Yahweh is not impatient!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 2:7
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Prophets (ID: `group:GenericProphets`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Micah 2:7 (#6) (id: en_tn_MIC_m0zq)

אֵ֖לֶּה מַעֲלָלָ֑י⁠ו

**"Are these his deeds"**

Micah is using the question form to affirm that such calamities are not the kind of thing Yahweh normally does. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[These are not his deeds!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 2:7
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Prophets (ID: `group:GenericProphets`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Micah 2:7 (#7) (id: en_tn_MIC_wzg5)

אֵ֖לֶּה מַעֲלָלָ֑י⁠ו

**"Are these his deeds"**

The pronoun **his** refers to Yahweh. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[Are these Yahweh’s deeds]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 2:7
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Prophets (ID: `group:GenericProphets`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Micah 2:7 (#8) (id: en_tn_MIC_nvh2)

הֲ⁠ל֤וֹא דְבָרַ֨⁠י יֵיטִ֔יבוּ עִ֖ם הַ⁠יָּשָׁ֥ר הוֹלֵֽךְ

**"Do not my words do good with the one walking uprightly"**

Micah is using the question form to affirm that his words which are reflecting Yahweh’s words do good to those who live uprightly. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[Surely my words do good with the one walking uprightly.]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 2:7
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Prophets (ID: `group:GenericProphets`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Micah 2:7 (#9) (id: en_tn_MIC_g05w)

הַ⁠יָּשָׁ֥ר הוֹלֵֽךְ

**"the one walking uprightly"**

Micah is speaking of living or conducting one’s life as if it were **walking**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[the one who lives righteously]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

וְ⁠אֶתְמ֗וּל עַמִּ⁠י֙

**"But recently my people"**

This is likely the beginning of what Yahweh said. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could mark that this is a direct quote of Yahweh as modeled in the UST.

## Micah 2:8 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_lowl)

עַמִּ⁠י֙ לְ⁠אוֹיֵ֣ב יְקוֹמֵ֔ם

**"my people rise up as an enemy"**

The pronoun **my** refers to Yahweh, who begins speaking here about his own people. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[Yahweh’s people rise up as an enemy]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 2:8
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 2:8 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_qzp6)

עַמִּ⁠י֙ לְ⁠אוֹיֵ֣ב יְקוֹמֵ֔ם

**"my people rise up as an enemy"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **an enemy** rises up to attack, so the leaders of Israel who Yahweh calls **my people**, have turned against Yahweh and have attacked their own defenseless people. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[my people attack the way an enemy would attack]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 2:8
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Safety (ID: `keyterm:Safety`); Cloak (ID: `realia:Cloak`)

## Micah 2:8 (#4) (id: en_tn_MIC_snan)

תַּפְשִׁט֑וּ⁠ן

**"You strip"**

The pronoun **You** here and in the next verse, refers to the leaders of the Israelite people whom Yahweh has just described as rising up as an enemy against their own people. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[You leaders of my people strip]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 2:8
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 2:10 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_uw78)

תְּחַבֵּ֖ל

**"it will destroy"**

The phrase **it will destroy** is a translation of the standard written Hebrew text. This reading, however, does not seem to make sense. You may want to follow an alternative reading that scholars have proposed was original, pronouncing the consonants with different vowels. Alternate translation: \[it will be destroyed]

## Micah 2:11 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_in47)

לוּ־אִ֞ישׁ הֹלֵ֥ךְ ר֨וּחַ֙ וָ⁠שֶׁ֣קֶר כִּזֵּ֔ב

**"If a man walking {in} wind and falsehood lied"**

Micah is presenting a possible condition, that a person might come speaking lies and promising indulgence. Use a natural form in your language for introducing a condition that could happen. Alternate translation: \[Suppose a man walking in wind and falsehood lied]

See: Connect — Hypothetical Conditions

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 2:11

ר֨וּחַ֙ וָ⁠שֶׁ֣קֶר

**"in} wind and falsehood"**

The phrase **wind and falsehood** expresses a single idea. The word **wind** tells more about **falsehood**, describing it as empty and worthless. If it would be more natural in your language, you could express this meaning in a different way. Alternate translation: \[in worthless lies]

**"he would be the dripper of this people"**

Micah is speaking of the false prophet speaking these prophesies that the people want to hear as if he were the **dripper** whose words would fall like rain. ## Micah 2:12 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_tvi9)

אָסֹ֨ף אֶאֱסֹ֜ף

**"Gathering, I will gather"**

Yahweh is repeating forms of the verb **gather**, in order to intensify his expression of the idea that it conveys. If your language can repeat words for intensification, it would be appropriate to do that here in your translation. If not, your language may have another way of expressing the intensification. Alternate translation: \[I will certainly gather]

See: Reduplication

Yahweh is repeating forms of the verb **assemble**, in order to intensify his expression of the idea that it conveys. **"I will set them"**

## Micah 2:12 (#6) (id: en_tn_MIC_pl4h)

כְּ⁠צֹ֣אן בָּצְרָ֑ה כְּ⁠עֵ֨דֶר֙ בְּ⁠ת֣וֹךְ הַ⁠דָּֽבְר֔⁠וֹ

**"like sheep of Bozrah, like a flock in the midst of its pasture"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **sheep** are gathered together safely into **a flock** within their **pasture**, so Yahweh will gather his people together. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[like sheep of Bozrah gathered safely together, like a flock kept secure within its pasture]

* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationManual`)

הַ⁠פֹּרֵץ֙

**"The breaker"**

Here Yahweh is speaking of the leader who goes ahead of the people as if he were **The breaker**, one who breaks open a way through a barrier. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[The one who breaks through]

## Micah 2:13 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_grmn)

עָלָ֤ה \& לִ⁠פְנֵי⁠הֶ֔ם

**"has gone up to the face of them"**

Here, **to the face of them** represents being in front of them or ahead of them. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[has gone up in front of them]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 2:13
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 2:13 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_ghtc)

**"And their king has passed on to the face of them"**

Here again, **to the face of them** represents being in front of them, leading them. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[And their king has gone ahead of them]

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 2:13
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationManual`)

**"and Yahweh {is} at their head"**

* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Micah - Chapter 3 Introduction (id: en_tn_MIC_wy5w)

Micah 3 Chapter Introduction
============================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

Yahweh judges corrupt leaders and false prophets.

1. Yahweh condemns the injustice of Israel’s leaders (1–4\)
2. Yahweh exposes the deception of false prophets (5–8\)
3. Yahweh announces judgment on Jerusalem’s rulers (9–12\)

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### Justice

An unjust society was considered to be against the law of Moses. If these kingdoms did not have justice, they were not obedient to Yahweh.

Translation Issues in This Chapter
----------------------------------

### Metaphors

There are vivid pictures used in this chapter which show how the leaders of the people were treating the Israelites. These situations are absurd unless taken as a metaphor.

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 3:0


## Micah 3:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_qf6t)

וָ⁠אֹמַ֗ר

**"And I said"**

Micah is using the words **And I said** to introduce a direct quotation of what he said to the leaders. In your translation, use a natural way of introducing direct quotations in your language.

See: Quotations and Quote Margins

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 3:1
* **Associated Articles:** Quotations and Quote Margins (ID: `33426@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Micah 3:1 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_h5hq)

רָאשֵׁ֣י יַעֲקֹ֔ב

**"heads of Jacob"**

Here, **heads** represents the leaders of the people and the name **Jacob** the people of Israel who were all descendants of the patriarch Jacob, who was also known as Israel.. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[leaders of the people of Israel]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 3:1
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Micah 3:1 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_gar4)

בֵּ֣ית יִשְׂרָאֵ֑ל

**"the house of Israel"**

Here, **house** means all the people descended from a particular person. It envisions them as if they were one household living together. So **the house of Israel** figuratively means all the people descended from the patriarch Jacob, who was also known as Israel. Alternate translation: \[the people of Israel]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 3:1
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Micah 3:1 (#4) (id: en_tn_MIC_xcrv)

הֲ⁠ל֣וֹא לָ⁠כֶ֔ם לָ⁠דַ֖עַת אֶת־הַ⁠מִּשְׁפָּֽט

**"Is it not for you to know justice"**

Micah is using the question form to rebuke the leaders and to assert that they are obligated to know justice. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[It is certainly your responsibility to know justice!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 3:1
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

אֶת־הַ⁠מִּשְׁפָּֽט

**"justice"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **justice**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[how to act justly]

ט֖וֹב וְ⁠אֹ֣הֲבֵי רעה

**"good and lovers of evil"**

Micah is using the adjectives **good** and **evil** as nouns to mean certain kinds of actions. Your language may use adjectives in the same way. If not, you can translate this adjective with an equivalent phrase. Alternate translation: \[doing good things and love doing evil things]

## Micah 3:2 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_icw2)

גֹּזְלֵ֤י עוֹרָ⁠ם֙ מֵֽ⁠עֲלֵי⁠הֶ֔ם וּ⁠שְׁאֵרָ֖⁠ם מֵ⁠עַ֥ל עַצְמוֹתָֽ⁠ם

**"tearing their skin from upon them and their flesh from upon their bones"**

Here Micah is speaking of the way the leaders oppress the people as if the leaders were butchers **tearing their skin from upon them and their flesh from upon their bones**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[brutally oppressing the people, taking everything they have]

**"their skin from upon them and their flesh from upon their bones"**

וְ⁠עוֹרָ⁠ם֙ מֵ⁠עֲלֵי⁠הֶ֣ם הִפְשִׁ֔יטוּ וְ⁠אֶת־עַצְמֹֽתֵי⁠הֶ֖ם פִּצֵּ֑חוּ

## Micah 3:3 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_ymf7)

הִפְשִׁ֔יטוּ וְ⁠אֶת־עַצְמֹֽתֵי⁠הֶ֖ם פִּצֵּ֑חוּ וּ⁠פָרְשׂוּ֙

**"they have stripped, and their bones they have broken, and they have chopped {them} up"**

Even though Micah is addressing the leaders of Israel directly (see 3:1\), here he is speaking of them in the third person as **they**. If it would be helpful in your language, you could translate this in the second person for consistency. Alternate translation: \[you have stripped, and their bones you have broken, and you have chopped them up]

## Micah 3:3 (#4) (id: en_tn_MIC_bygr)

וּ⁠פָרְשׂוּ֙ כַּ⁠אֲשֶׁ֣ר בַּ⁠סִּ֔יר וּ⁠כְ⁠בָשָׂ֖ר בְּ⁠ת֥וֹךְ קַלָּֽחַת

**"and they have chopped {them} up as {what} is in the pot, and as flesh inside a cauldron"**

The point of this comparison is that the leaders treat the people as cruelly as a cook treats the meat that he chops up to cook **as what is in the pot** and **as flesh inside a cauldron**. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[and they have chopped them up as cruelly as a cook chops up the meat that goes in the pot and the flesh inside a cauldron]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 3:3
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Cooking Pot (ID: `realia:CookingPot`)

וְ⁠יַסְתֵּ֨ר פָּנָ֤י⁠ו מֵ⁠הֶם֙ בָּ⁠עֵ֣ת הַ⁠הִ֔יא כַּ⁠אֲשֶׁ֥ר הֵרֵ֖עוּ מַעַלְלֵי⁠הֶֽם

## Micah 3:6 (#5) (id: en_tn_MIC_bg9i)

וּ⁠בָ֤אָה הַ⁠שֶּׁ֨מֶשׁ֙ עַל־הַ⁠נְּבִיאִ֔ים וְ⁠קָדַ֥ר עֲלֵי⁠הֶ֖ם הַ⁠יּֽוֹם

**"And the sun will go down on the prophets, and the day will become dark over them"**

Yahweh is speaking of the prophets losing his favor and guidance and the gift of true prophesy as if the **sun will go down on the prophets**, and **the day will become dark over them**, leaving them in darkness. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[And the prophets will lose my guidance and the ability to see my revelation]

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 3:6

עַל־הַ⁠נְּבִיאִ֔ים וְ⁠קָדַ֥ר עֲלֵי⁠הֶ֖ם הַ⁠יּֽוֹם

Even though Yahweh is addressing the prophets directly, here he is speaking of them in the third person as **the prophets** and **them**. ## Micah 3:7 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_thsg)

וְ⁠חָֽפְרוּ֙ הַ⁠קֹּ֣סְמִ֔ים

**"and the diviners will be disgraced"**

The expression **will be disgraced** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[and the diviners will no longer have honor from the people because they are unable to predict the future]

## Micah 3:7 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_afk4)

וְ⁠עָט֥וּ עַל־שָׂפָ֖ם כֻּלָּ֑⁠ם

**"And all of them will cover over the mustache"**

Covering the upper lip, represented here by **cover over the mustache**, was a symbolic action by which people showed that they were unclean or in mourning. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could explain the significance of this action in the text of your translation or in a footnote. Alternate translation: \[And all of them will cover their upper lip to express their sorrow and shame]

See: Symbolic Action

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 3:7
* **Associated Articles:** Symbolic Action (ID: `33402@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 3:7 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_wyvz)

מַעֲנֵ֖ה אֱלֹהִֽים

**"answer of God"**

Here Micah is using the possessive form to describe an **answer** that comes from **God**. If a speaker of your language would not use the possessive form for this, you could express this idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[answer from God]

## Micah 3:8 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_psx2)

וְ⁠אוּלָ֗ם אָנֹכִ֞י מָלֵ֤אתִי

**"But I, in contrast, I am full of"**

Micah uses the words **But** and **in contrast** here to indicate a contrast between the false prophets, who will be put to shame, and himself, who is empowered by the Spirit of Yahweh. In your translation, indicate this contrast in a way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[I, however, am full of]

See: Connect — Contrast Relationship

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 3:8
* **Associated Articles:** Connect — Contrast Relationship (ID: `33329@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 3:8 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_a4cr)

וְ⁠אוּלָ֗ם אָנֹכִ֞י מָלֵ֤אתִי

**"But I, in contrast, I am full of"**

Micah is stating the pronoun **I** separately, even though the verb translated as **I am full** already includes this meaning. He is doing that to emphasize how he is different from the other prophets mentioned previously. If a speaker of your language would use an explicit pronoun for the same purpose, you may want to use that construction in your translation. If not, your language may have other ways of showing the meaning here. Alternate translation: \[In contrast to them, I am full]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

## Micah - Chapter 4 Introduction (id: en_tn_MIC_pzv8)

Micah 4 Chapter Introduction
============================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

Yahweh promises the future glory and restoration of his people.

1. Yahweh establishes his kingdom among the nations (1–5\)
2. Yahweh gathers and restores his people (6–8\)
3. Yahweh delivers Zion through present suffering (9–13\)

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### The remnant

Micah speaks about a future hope and a remnant who will return to their homeland. Verses 1–8 are a vision of the future when God will restore Israel to its former glory. These verses will give hope to those about to be removed from their homeland.

Translation Issues in This Chapter
----------------------------------

### Similar passage

Notice that Micah 4:1–3 is very similar to Isaiah 2:2–4\. You may want to translate many of these phrases in a similar way.

### Daughter Zion

Yahweh is addressing the people of Jerusalem as “daughter Zion” whom he is personifying as a woman—crying out, in labor, then threshing her enemies.

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:0


## Micah 4:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_knb7)

יִ֠הְיֶה הַ֣ר בֵּית־יְהוָ֤ה נָכוֹן֙ בְּ⁠רֹ֣אשׁ הֶ⁠הָרִ֔ים וְ⁠נִשָּׂ֥א ה֖וּא מִ⁠גְּבָע֑וֹת

**"the mountain of the house of Yahweh will be established as the head of the mountains. And it will be lifted above the hills"**

These two clauses mean basically the same thing. The second emphasizes the meaning of the first by repeating the same idea with different words. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could combine them and express the emphasis in another way. Alternate translation: \[Yahweh will make the mountain where his temple stands become more important than all the other hills and mountains]

See: Parallelism

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:1
* **Associated Articles:** Parallelism (ID: `33299@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

**"the mountain of the house of Yahweh will be established"**

The expression **will be established** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. If you need to say who does the action, the context indicates that it is Yahweh. Alternate translation: \[Yahweh will establish the mountain of his house]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Micah 4:1 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_vge6)

הַ֣ר בֵּית־יְהוָ֤ה

**"the mountain of the house of Yahweh"**

Here Micah is speaking of the temple as if it were a **house**, since it was the place where Yahweh was present among his people. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[the mountain on which the temple of Yahweh stands]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:1
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Micah 4:1 (#4) (id: en_tn_MIC_tdh8)

בְּ⁠רֹ֣אשׁ הֶ⁠הָרִ֔ים

**"as the head of the mountains"**

Micah is speaking of the mountain where his temple stands as if it were **the head of the mountains**. He means that Yahweh will exalt it above all the others so that it becomes the most important one. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[as the most prominent and important of the mountains]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:1
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Micah 4:1 (#5) (id: en_tn_MIC_grr1)

וְ⁠נִשָּׂ֥א ה֖וּא

**"And it will be lifted"**

The expression **will be lifted** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. If you need to say who does the action, the context indicates that it is Yahweh. Alternate translation: \[And Yahweh will lift it]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:1
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Micah 4:1 (#6) (id: en_tn_MIC_j8nr)

וְ⁠נָהֲר֥וּ עָלָ֖י⁠ו עַמִּֽים

**"and peoples will stream to it"**

Micah is speaking of the peoples as if they were water that could **stream** to the mountain. He means that they will come to it together in great numbers, like a river flowing. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and peoples will come to it together in great numbers]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:1
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Micah 4:2 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_b6id)

הַר־יְהוָ֗ה

**"the mountain of Yahweh"**

By **the mountain of Yahweh**, the speakers are referring to Mount Zion in Jerusalem, where the temple of Yahweh was located. You could indicate this explicitly in your translation if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[Mount Zion]

## Micah 4:2 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_ncir)

בֵּית֙ אֱלֹהֵ֣י יַעֲקֹ֔ב

**"the house of the God of Jacob"**

Here Micah is speaking of the temple as if it were a **house**, since it was the place where Yahweh was present among his people. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[the temple of the God of Jacob]

## Micah 4:2 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_xb9v)

אֱלֹהֵ֣י יַעֲקֹ֔ב

**"the God of Jacob"**

Micah is not using the possessive form **the God of Jacob** to indicate that Jacob possesses or controls God. Rather he is using these words to indicate that God is the one whom Jacob worshiped as modeled in the UST.

See: Possession

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:2
* **Associated Articles:** Possession (ID: `33303@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 4:2 (#4) (id: en_tn_MIC_iakn)

וְ⁠יוֹרֵ֨⁠נוּ֙ מִ⁠דְּרָכָ֔י⁠ו וְ⁠נֵלְכָ֖ה בְּ⁠אֹֽרְחֹתָ֑י⁠ו

**"so that he will teach us from his ways, and so that we may walk in his paths"**

Micah is speaking of how Yahweh wants people to live as if it were **his ways** and **his paths** that they could walk along. He means that to walk in Yahweh’s paths is to live in the way that he directs. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[so that he will teach us what he requires, and so that we may live as he directs]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:2
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Instruction (ID: `keyterm:Instruction`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); The Way (ID: `keyterm:TheWay`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Micah 4:2 (#5) (id: en_tn_MIC_n8yy)

וּ⁠דְבַר־יְהוָ֖ה מִ⁠ירוּשָׁלִָֽם

**"and the word of Yahweh from Jerusalem"**

Micah is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[and the word of Yahweh will go out from Jerusalem]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:2
* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Instruction (ID: `keyterm:Instruction`); Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); The Way (ID: `keyterm:TheWay`); Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Word of the Lord (ID: `keyterm:WordOfTheLord`); House (ID: `realia:House`)

## Micah 4:2 (#6) (id: en_tn_MIC_fak7)

כִּ֤י מִ⁠צִּיּוֹן֙ תֵּצֵ֣א תוֹרָ֔ה וּ⁠דְבַר־יְהוָ֖ה מִ⁠ירוּשָׁלִָֽם

**"For from Zion the law will go out, and the word of Yahweh from Jerusalem"**

These two clauses mean basically the same thing. The second emphasizes the meaning of the first by repeating the same idea with different words. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could combine them and express the emphasis in another way. Alternate translation: \[For the word of Yahweh will surely go out from Jerusalem]

See: Parallelism

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:2
* **Associated Articles:** Parallelism (ID: `33299@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 4:2 (#7) (id: en_tn_MIC_n9ki)

וּ⁠דְבַר־יְהוָ֖ה

**"and the word of Yahweh"**

Micah using the term **the word of Yahweh** to represent what Yahweh said by using words. If it would be clearer in your language, you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and what Yahweh has spoken will go out]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:2
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 4:3 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_tm9b)

וְ⁠שָׁפַ֗ט

**"And he will judge"**

The pronoun **he** refers to Yahweh. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[And Yahweh will judge]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:3
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 4:3 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_yive)

וְ⁠כִתְּת֨וּ

**"And they will beat"**

The pronoun **they** refers to the many peoples whom Yahweh will judge. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[And these many peoples will beat]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:3
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 4:3 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_k29q)

חַרְבֹתֵי⁠הֶ֜ם לְ⁠אִתִּ֗ים וַ⁠חֲנִיתֹֽתֵי⁠הֶם֙ לְ⁠מַזְמֵר֔וֹת לֹֽא־יִשְׂא֞וּ גּ֤וֹי אֶל־גּוֹי֙ חֶ֔רֶב

**"their swords into plowshares and their spears into pruning hooks. Nation will not lift up sword against nation"**

Here, **sword** and **spears** represent warfare and armed conflict. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[their weapons of war into farming tools. Nation will not make war against nation]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:3
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Pruning Knife (ID: `realia:PruningKnife`); Sword (ID: `realia:Sword`); Spear (ID: `realia:Spear`); Mattock (ID: `realia:Mattock`)

## Micah 4:4 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_wlun)

וְ⁠יָשְׁב֗וּ אִ֣ישׁ תַּ֧חַת גַּפְנ֛⁠וֹ וְ⁠תַ֥חַת תְּאֵנָת֖⁠וֹ וְ⁠אֵ֣ין מַחֲרִ֑יד

**"And they will sit, each person under his vine and under his fig tree. And there will be no terrorizer"**

Here, **under his vine and under his fig tree** represents living peacefully and safely on their own land. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[And they will dwell, each person safely and prosperously on his own land, undisturbed by anyone]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:4
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`); Fig (ID: `flora:Fig`)

## Micah 4:4 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_ofut)

פִ֛י יְהוָ֥ה צְבָא֖וֹת דִּבֵּֽר

**"the mouth of Yahweh of Armies has spoken"**

Micah is using **the mouth of Yahweh** to represent Yahweh himself in the act of speaking. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression from your culture or state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[Yahweh of Armies himself has spoken]

See: Synecdoche

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:4
* **Associated Articles:** Synecdoche (ID: `33315@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`); Fig (ID: `flora:Fig`)

## Micah 4:5 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_cfjp)

כִּ֚י כָּל־הָ֣⁠עַמִּ֔ים יֵלְכ֕וּ אִ֖ישׁ בְּ⁠שֵׁ֣ם אֱלֹהָ֑י⁠ו

**"For all the peoples walk, each one, in the name of his god"**

Here, the word **walk** is used to describe a habitual lifestyle. In this context, to **walk … in the name of his god** describes the people worshiping and obeying their own false gods. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[For all the peoples follow after their own gods] or \[For all the peoples serve their own gods]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:5
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Micah 4:5 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_t125)

בְּ⁠שֵׁ֣ם אֱלֹהָ֑י⁠ו

**"in the name of his god"**

Here, **name** represents the authority and character of a god. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[under the authority of his god]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:5
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Micah 4:5 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_i4b1)

וַ⁠אֲנַ֗חְנוּ נֵלֵ֛ךְ

**"But we, we will walk"**

Micah is stating the pronoun **we** separately, even though the verb translated as **we will walk** already includes this meaning. He is doing that to emphasize the contrast between what the other nations do and what he and his people will do. If a speaker of your language would use an explicit pronoun for the same purpose, you may want to use that construction in your translation. If not, your language may have other ways of showing the meaning here. Alternate translation: \[However, my people and I will walk]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:5
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Micah 4:5 (#4) (id: en_tn_MIC_ebn3)

נֵלֵ֛ךְ בְּ⁠שֵׁם־יְהוָ֥ה אֱלֹהֵ֖י⁠נוּ

**"we will walk in the name of Yahweh our God"**

Again, the word **walk** is used to describe a habitual lifestyle. In contrast to the first phrase in this verse, to **we will walk in the name of Yahweh** describes the Israelites worshiping and obeying the true God, Yahweh. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[we will live in the manner that Yahweh our God wants]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:5
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Micah 4:5 (#5) (id: en_tn_MIC_bcto)

יְהוָ֥ה אֱלֹהֵ֖י⁠נוּ

**"Yahweh our God"**

Micah is not using this possessive form **our God** to indicate that the Israelites possess or control Yahweh. Rather, they are using these words to indicate that Yahweh is the God whom the Israelites worship, including himself. Alternate translation: \[Yahweh, the God whom we worship]

See: Possession

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:5
* **Associated Articles:** Possession (ID: `33303@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Micah 4:6 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_f4t8)

אֹֽסְפָה֙ הַ⁠צֹּ֣לֵעָ֔ה וְ⁠הַ⁠נִּדָּחָ֖ה אֲקַבֵּ֑צָה

**"I shall assemble the lame and gather the outcast"**

Yahweh is speaking of the people of Israel as if they were a flock of sheep, some of them **lame** and some of them **outcast**, whom he gathers together. He is portraying himself as a shepherd who cares for his scattered and suffering people. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[I shall bring together my people who are weak and bring back those who were driven away]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:6
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Micah 4:6 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_nhtz)

אֹֽסְפָה֙ הַ⁠צֹּ֣לֵעָ֔ה וְ⁠הַ⁠נִּדָּחָ֖ה אֲקַבֵּ֑צָה

**"I shall assemble the lame and gather the outcast"**

Yahweh is using the adjectives **the lame** and **the outcast** as nouns to mean certain kinds of people. Your language may use adjectives in the same way. If not, you can translate these adjectives with an equivalent phrase. Alternate translation: \[I shall assemble those who are lame, and gather those who were outcast]

See: Nominal Adjectives

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:6
* **Associated Articles:** Nominal Adjectives (ID: `33294@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Micah 4:6 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_yucb)

הַ⁠צֹּ֣לֵעָ֔ה וְ⁠הַ⁠נִּדָּחָ֖ה אֲקַבֵּ֑צָה

**"the lame and gather the outcast"**

Yahweh is using the adjectives **the lame** and **the outcast** as nouns to mean certain kinds of people. Your language may use adjectives in the same way. If not, you can translate these adjectives with an equivalent phrase. Alternate translation: \[the people who are lame and gather the people who have been cast out]

See: Nominal Adjectives

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:6
* **Associated Articles:** Nominal Adjectives (ID: `33294@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Micah 4:6 (#4) (id: en_tn_MIC_cgs7)

וַ⁠אֲשֶׁ֖ר הֲרֵעֹֽתִי

**"and those whom I have afflicted"**

Yahweh is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[and I will gather those whom I have afflicted]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:6
* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Micah 4:6 (#5) (id: en_tn_MIC_npvz)

וַ⁠אֲשֶׁ֖ר הֲרֵעֹֽתִי

**"and those whom I have afflicted"**

Micah assumes that readers will know that **those whom I have afflicted** would refer to those who survived the previous judgment he predicted Yahweh would bring in the earlier portions. You could say that explicitly if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[in fact, I will gather all those who survive my discipline]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:6
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Micah 4:7 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_czgk)

לִ⁠שְׁאֵרִ֔ית

**"into a remnant"**

By **remnant**, Yahweh means those few people who he will allow to survive his discipline when he judges the sins of his people. You could indicate this explicitly in your translation if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[into a people who will survive]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:7
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Micah 4:7 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_kbsn)

יְהוָ֤ה

**"Yahweh"**

Yahweh is speaking of himself in the third person as **Yahweh**. If it would be helpful in your language, you could translate this in the first person as modeled in the UST.

See: First, Second or Third Person

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:7
* **Associated Articles:** First, Second or Third Person (ID: `33256@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Micah 4:8 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_q0b9)

מִגְדַּל־עֵ֗דֶר

**"watchtower for the flock"**

Yahweh is speaking of Jerusalem as if it were a **watchtower** from which a shepherd watches over his flock. He means that the city has the role of protecting and overseeing the people of God. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[protector of my people]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:8
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Tower of Eder (ID: `place:TowerOfEder`)

## Micah 4:8 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_w0es)

בַּת־צִיּ֖וֹן

**"daughter Zion"**

Yahweh is using **daughter Zion** as a common expression of the culture to mean “the people of Jerusalem.” He refers to Jerusalem in the same way at the end of this verse. If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[the people of Jerusalem]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:8
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Tower of Eder (ID: `place:TowerOfEder`)

## Micah 4:8 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_wvzx)

עָדֶ֣י⁠ךָ תֵּאתֶ֑ה וּ⁠בָאָ֗ה הַ⁠מֶּמְשָׁלָה֙ הָ⁠רִ֣אשֹׁנָ֔ה

**"to you it will arrive, and the former dominion will come"**

These two clauses mean basically the same thing. The second emphasizes the meaning of the first by repeating the same idea with different words. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could combine them and express the emphasis in another way as in the UST. Another approach would be to connect the phrases with a word other than **and** in order to show that the second phrase is repeating the first one, not saying something additional. Alternate translation: \[your former dominion will come, indeed to you it will arrive]

See: Parallelism

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:8
* **Associated Articles:** Parallelism (ID: `33299@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Jerusalem (ID: `place:Jerusalem`); Tower of Eder (ID: `place:TowerOfEder`)

## Micah 4:8 (#4) (id: en_tn_MIC_uzt1)

הַ⁠מֶּמְשָׁלָה֙ הָ⁠רִ֣אשֹׁנָ֔ה

**"the former dominion"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **dominion**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[the right to rule as before]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:8
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 4:9 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_i9y2)

לָ֥⁠מָּה תָרִ֖יעִי רֵ֑עַ

**"why do you shout a shout"**

Micah is using the question form to challenge the people of Jerusalem about their loud crying. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[you are shouting loudly!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:9
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationManual`)


## Micah 4:9 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_awhr)

תָרִ֖יעִי רֵ֑עַ

**"do you shout a shout"**

Micah is repeating forms of the verb **shout** in order to intensify his expression of the idea that it conveys. If your language can repeat words for intensification, it would be appropriate to do that here in your translation. If not, your language may have another way of expressing the intensification. Alternate translation: \[are you crying out so loudly]

See: Reduplication

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:9


## Micah 4:9 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_cqng)

הֲ⁠מֶ֣לֶךְ אֵֽין־בָּ֗⁠ךְ אִֽם־יוֹעֲצֵ⁠ךְ֙ אָבָ֔ד כִּֽי־הֶחֱזִיקֵ֥⁠ךְ חִ֖יל כַּ⁠יּוֹלֵדָֽה

**"Is there no king among you, or has your counselor perished, that pain grips you like a woman giving birth"**

Micah is using the question form to prompt the people to recognize that they still have a king and a counselor to guide them and thus should not be crying out. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[Since you have a king among you, and your counselor has not perished, you do not need to cry out like you are in as much pain as a woman giving birth.]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:9
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationManual`)


## Micah 4:9 (#4) (id: en_tn_MIC_fxqp)

הֶחֱזִיקֵ֥⁠ךְ חִ֖יל כַּ⁠יּוֹלֵדָֽה

**"pain grips you like a woman giving birth"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **a woman giving birth** suffers severe pain, so the people of Jerusalem will experience the same intense anguish. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[pain grips you as severely as the pain of a woman in labor]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:9
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationManual`)


## Micah 4:9 (#5) (id: en_tn_MIC_snwn)

חִ֖יל

**"pain"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **pain**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[painful spasms]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:9
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationManual`)


## Micah 4:10 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_ns71)

ח֧וּלִי וָ⁠גֹ֛חִי בַּת־צִיּ֖וֹן כַּ⁠יּֽוֹלֵדָ֑ה

**"Writhe in pain and burst forth, daughter Zion, like a woman giving birth"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **a woman giving birth** cries out and writhes in labor, so the people of Jerusalem will agonize in their distress. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. תִּנָּצֵ֔לִי

**"you will be rescued"**

The expression **you will be rescued** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. If you need to say who does the action, the context indicates that it is Yahweh. Alternate translation: \[Yahweh will be the one who rescues you]

**"many nations are assembled against you"**

The expression **are assembled** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[many nations have gathered together against you]

## Micah 4:11 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_hd3m)

**"Let her be defiled and let our eyes gaze"**

The people of the other nations are saying what they want to happen to Jerusalem. See the discussion in the introduction to this book about “let” and “may” and follow the decision of your team of how a speakers will express in this context what they want to happen. Alternate translation: \[We want her to be defiled and we want our eyes to gaze]

## Micah 4:11 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_suie)

וְ⁠תַ֥חַז בְּ⁠צִיּ֖וֹן עֵינֵֽי⁠נוּ

**"and let our eyes gaze on Zion"**

The nations are using the expression **our eyes gaze** as a common expression of the culture to mean “we will gloat over.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and we want to look with satisfaction on Zion’s downfall]

## Micah 4:12 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_li4d)

וְ⁠הֵ֗מָּה לֹ֤א יָֽדְעוּ֙

**"But they, they do not know"**

Yahweh is stating the pronoun **they** separately, even though the verb translated as **they do not know** already includes this meaning. He is doing that to emphasize how ignorant the people of the other nations are of his thoughts. If a speaker of your language would use an explicit pronoun for the same purpose, you may want to use that construction in your translation. If not, your language may have other ways of showing the meaning here. Alternate translation: \[But of course, they do not know]

**"he has gathered them like bundles of grain for the threshing floor"**

If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[he has gathered them together so that he can destroy them, just as a farmer gathers bundles of grain to the threshing floor to be crushed]

## Micah 4:13 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_wv0i)

ק֧וּמִי וָ⁠ד֣וֹשִׁי בַת־צִיּ֗וֹן

**"Arise and thresh, daughter Zion"**

Yahweh is speaking of the people of Jerusalem, or **daughter Zion,** defeating her enemies as if she were an ox that could **thresh** grain. He means that Jerusalem will crush the nations as an ox crushes grain on the threshing floor. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[Arise and crush your enemies, people of Jerusalem]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:13

## Micah 4:13 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_mpzi)

קַרְנֵ֞⁠ךְ אָשִׂ֤ים בַּרְזֶל֙ וּ⁠פַרְסֹתַ֨יִ⁠ךְ֙ אָשִׂ֣ים נְחוּשָׁ֔ה

**"I will make your horn iron, and I will make your hooves bronze"**

These two clauses mean basically the same thing. The second emphasizes the meaning of the first by repeating the same idea with different words. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could combine them and express the emphasis in another way as in the UST. Another approach would be to connect the phrases with a word other than **and** in order to show that the second phrase is repeating the first one, not saying something additional. Alternate translation: \[I will make your horn iron, indeed I will make your hooves bronze]

See: Parallelism

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 4:13
* **Associated Articles:** Parallelism (ID: `33299@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 4:13 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_osef)

קַרְנֵ֞⁠ךְ אָשִׂ֤ים בַּרְזֶל֙

**"I will make your horn iron"**

Yahweh is speaking of the strength he will give Zion as if it were a **horn** made of **iron**. An animal’s horn represents power, and iron represents strength that cannot be broken. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[I will make you unbreakably strong]

## Micah 4:13 (#4) (id: en_tn_MIC_najr)

וּ⁠פַרְסֹתַ֨יִ⁠ךְ֙ אָשִׂ֣ים נְחוּשָׁ֔ה

**"and I will make your hooves bronze"**

Yahweh continues to speak of Zion as if it were a powerful threshing ox whose **hooves** he would make of **bronze**. He means that he will make Zion strong enough to trample and crush her enemies. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and I will make you strong enough to trample your enemies]

## Micah - Chapter 5 Introduction (id: en_tn_MIC_y1t7)

Micah 5 Chapter Introduction
============================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

Yahweh promises a ruler who will shepherd and deliver his people.

1. Yahweh raises up a ruler from Bethlehem (1–6\)
2. Yahweh preserves his people among the nations (7–9\)
3. Yahweh removes all sources of false security and idolatry (10–15\)

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### Messiah

This chapter contains a prophecy explaining that the Messiah was to be born in Bethlehem. This prophecy is fulfilled by Jesus’s birth and referenced in the New Testament in each of the gospel accounts of that event.

Translation Issues in This Chapter
----------------------------------

### Change in Personal Pronouns

Extra care must be taken in translating pronouns in this chapter. Sometimes “I” is a reference to the author and other times it is a reference to Yahweh. There is also a noticeable shift where the author stops speaking about the people as “you” and begins speaking about them as “we” or “us.”

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 5:0


## Micah 5:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_l094)

בַּ⁠שֵּׁ֨בֶט֙ יַכּ֣וּ עַֽל־הַ⁠לְּחִ֔י

**"with the rod they strike on the cheek"**

Micah is using **with the rod they strike on the cheek** as a common expression of the culture to mean “they insult and humiliate him.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[they utterly humiliate]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 5:1
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Under Siege (ID: `keyterm:StateOfBeingUnderSiege`); Rod (ID: `realia:Rod`); Staff (ID: `realia:Staff`)

## Micah 5:1 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_w8h0)

בַת־גְּד֔וּד

**"daughter troop"**

Micah is speaking of the surviving remnant of the people of Jerusalem (whom he previously called “daughter Zion”) as if they were a **daughter troop**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly as modeled in the UST.

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 5:1

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Under Siege (ID: `keyterm:StateOfBeingUnderSiege`); Rod (ID: `realia:Rod`); Staff (ID: `realia:Staff`)

מָצ֖וֹר שָׂ֣ם עָלֵ֑י⁠נוּ

**"He has set a siege against us"**

The pronoun **He** refers to the enemy who is attacking Jerusalem. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[Our enemy has laid siege against us]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 5:1
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); Under Siege (ID: `keyterm:StateOfBeingUnderSiege`); Rod (ID: `realia:Rod`); Staff (ID: `realia:Staff`)

## Micah 5:1 (#4) (id: en_tn_MIC_ofw3)

עָלֵ֑י⁠נוּ

**"against us"**

By **us**, Micah means himself and the people of Jerusalem to whom he is speaking, so use the inclusive form of that word in your translation if your language marks that distinction.

See: Exclusive and Inclusive ‘We’

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 5:1
* **Associated Articles:** Exclusive and Inclusive ‘We’ (ID: `33271@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 5:2 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_u8jw)

בֵּֽית־לֶ֣חֶם אֶפְרָ֗תָה

**"Bethlehem Ephrathah"**

**Bethlehem Ephrathah** is the name of a town located in a region of Judah (see it referenced in Matthew 2:6\). **Ephrathah** distinguished it from another town called “Bethlehem” in the region of Zebulun. It is also called “the city of David” in Luke 2:4, since it was where David, king of Israel, was born.

See: How to Translate Names

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 5:2
* **Associated Articles:** How to Translate Names (ID: `33391@UWTranslationManual`)

מִמְּ⁠ךָ֙ לִ֣⁠י יֵצֵ֔א

**"from you will come out for me"**

The pronoun **me** refers to Yahweh, who is speaking these words through Micah. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[from you will come out for Yahweh]

## Micah 5:2 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_utqw)

וּ⁠מוֹצָאֹתָ֥י⁠ו מִ⁠קֶּ֖דֶם

**"and his goings out {are} from aforetime"**

Micah is using **his goings out** as a common expression of the culture to mean “his origin.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and his origin is from ancient times]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 5:2
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationManual`)

מִ⁠ימֵ֥י עוֹלָֽם

**"from the days of eternity"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **eternity**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[from the days before time began]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationManual`)
