## Micah 5:3 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_gjcf)

יִתְּנֵ֔⁠ם

**"he will give them up"**

The pronoun **he** refers to Yahweh, and the pronoun **them** refers to the people of Israel. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[Yahweh will give the people of Israel up]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 5:3
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Micah 5:3 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_nu1x)

עַד־עֵ֥ת יוֹלֵדָ֖ה יָלָ֑דָה

**"until the time {when} the bearing {one} gives birth"**

This prophecy appears to have two fulfillments. The first fulfillment would be when God brings the people of Israel back to their land. In this case, **the bearing one** would refer to “daughter Zion” 4:10, who stands for the faithful remnant of Israel. She **gives birth** to the restored nation of Israel. The second and greater fulfillment is when the mother of the Messiah gives birth to him who is able to create the new, fully restored people of God, bringing together the remnant of Israel and all of **his brothers** redeemed from the rest of mankind. If possible, translate this in a way that can apply to both fulfillments.

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 5:3

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`)

## Micah 5:3 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_kfbu)

וְ⁠יֶ֣תֶר אֶחָ֔י⁠ו

**"and the remainder of his brothers"**

The pronoun **his** refers to the ruler whom Yahweh has promised. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[and the remainder of that ruler’s brothers]

וְ⁠יֶ֣תֶר אֶחָ֔י⁠ו

**"and the remainder of his brothers"**

Micah is speaking of the fellow Israelites of this ruler as if they were his **brothers**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and the remainder of his fellow Israelites]

The pronoun **he** refers to the ruler that Yahweh promised to raise up from Bethlehem, whom Micah described in the previous verses. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[And the ruler will stand]

**"in the majesty of the name of Yahweh his God"**

## Micah 5:5 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_cz0i)

בְ⁠אַרְצֵ֗⁠נוּ וְ⁠כִ֤י יִדְרֹךְ֙ בְּ⁠אַרְמְנֹתֵ֔י⁠נוּ

**"into our land, and when he treads on our citadels"**

**"seven shepherds and eight princes of man"**

## Micah 5:6 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_djnh)

וְ⁠רָע֞וּ

**"And they will shepherd"**

The pronoun **they** refers to the leaders mentioned in the previous verse who will fight against Assyria. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[And these leaders will shepherd]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 5:6
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 5:6 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_qrwv)

וְ⁠רָע֞וּ אֶת־אֶ֤רֶץ אַשּׁוּר֙ בַּ⁠חֶ֔רֶב

**"And they will shepherd the land of Assyria with the sword"**

Micah is speaking of these leaders ruling with military power as if they would **shepherd** them like a herd of sheep but in this context of the Assyrians, it would be **with a sword**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[And they will rule the land of Assyria with military power]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 5:6
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 5:6 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_t1l0)

וְ⁠אֶת־אֶ֥רֶץ נִמְרֹ֖ד בִּ⁠פְתָחֶ֑י⁠הָ

**"and the land of Nimrod in its entrances"**

Micah is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[and they will shepherd the land of Nimrod in its entrances]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 5:6
* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 5:6 (#4) (id: en_tn_MIC_ww49)

וְ⁠אֶת־אֶ֥רֶץ נִמְרֹ֖ד

**"and the land of Nimrod"**

Here, **the land of Nimrod** represents the territory that the ancient ruler Nimrod founded, that is, Assyria. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[which is the land that Nimrod founded,]

See: How to Translate Names

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 5:6
* **Associated Articles:** How to Translate Names (ID: `33391@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Nimrod (ID: `person:Nimrod`); Assyrians (ID: `group:Assyria`); Assyria (ID: `place:Assyria`); Sword (ID: `realia:Sword`)

## Micah 5:6 (#5) (id: en_tn_MIC_ecyp)

בִּ⁠פְתָחֶ֑י⁠הָ

**"in its entrances"**

The phrase **in its entrances** is a translation of the standard written Hebrew text. This reading, however, does not seem to make sense. Scholars have proposed that the original reading was a very similar word meaning “drawn sword,” which is represented in some ancient translations. You may want to follow this reading, or you may want to follow the major translation in your area. Alternate translation: \[with a drawn sword]

See: Alternative Readings

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 5:6

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Nimrod (ID: `person:Nimrod`); Assyrians (ID: `group:Assyria`); Assyria (ID: `place:Assyria`); Sword (ID: `realia:Sword`)

וְ⁠הִצִּיל֙ מֵֽ⁠אַשּׁ֔וּר

**"And he will deliver {us} from Assyria"**

The pronoun **he** refers to the ruler whom Yahweh has promised. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[And the promised ruler will rescue us from Assyria]

**"the remnant of Jacob"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **remnant**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[those of Jacob who remain]

* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 5:7 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_xbu4)

שְׁאֵרִ֣ית יַעֲקֹ֗ב

**"the remnant of Jacob"**

Here, **Jacob** represents the descendants of Jacob, that is, the people of Israel. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[the remnant of the people of Israel]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 5:7
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Grass (ID: `flora:Grass`)

## Micah 5:7 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_wnhn)

כְּ⁠טַל֙ מֵ⁠אֵ֣ת יְהוָ֔ה כִּ⁠רְבִיבִ֖ים עֲלֵי־עֵ֑שֶׂב

**"like dew from Yahweh, like showers on the grass"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **dew** and **showers** come from Yahweh and refresh the grass, so the survivors of Israel will be a blessing to the many peoples among whom they live. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[a blessing to them, like refreshing dew that comes from Yahweh, like showers on the grass]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 5:7
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Grass (ID: `flora:Grass`)

## Micah 5:7 (#4) (id: en_tn_MIC_wcml)

אֲשֶׁ֤ר לֹֽא־יְקַוֶּה֙ לְ⁠אִ֔ישׁ

**"that does not wait for a man"**

The pronoun **that** refers to the **dew** and the **showers**, which come from Yahweh rather than from any human effort. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[moisture that does not wait for a man]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 5:7
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Grass (ID: `flora:Grass`)

## Micah 5:7 (#5) (id: en_tn_MIC_cnrm)

אֲשֶׁ֤ר לֹֽא־יְקַוֶּה֙ לְ⁠אִ֔ישׁ וְ⁠לֹ֥א יְיַחֵ֖ל לִ⁠בְנֵ֥י אָדָֽם

**"that does not wait for a man and does not hope for the sons of man"**

These two clauses mean basically the same thing. The second emphasizes the meaning of the first by repeating the same idea with different words. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could combine them and express the emphasis in another way. Alternate translation: \[that does depend completely on Yahweh and not on people at all]

See: Parallelism

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 5:7
* **Associated Articles:** Parallelism (ID: `33299@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Grass (ID: `flora:Grass`)

## Micah 5:7 (#6) (id: en_tn_MIC_nrze)

לֹֽא־יְקַוֶּה֙ לְ⁠אִ֔ישׁ וְ⁠לֹ֥א יְיַחֵ֖ל לִ⁠בְנֵ֥י אָדָֽם

**"does not wait for a man and does not hope for the sons of man"**

Here, **a man** and **the sons of man** represent people in general, not specific individuals. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could use expressions that would be more natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[does not wait for anyone and does not hope for any human being]

See: Generic Noun Phrases

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 5:7
* **Associated Articles:** Generic Noun Phrases (ID: `33277@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Grass (ID: `flora:Grass`)

## Micah 5:8 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_xhgq)

כְּ⁠אַרְיֵה֙ בְּ⁠בַהֲמ֣וֹת יַ֔עַר כִּ⁠כְפִ֖יר בְּ⁠עֶדְרֵי צֹ֑אן

**"like a lion among the animals of the forest, like a young lion among the flocks of sheep"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **a lion** is stronger than the other animals and **a young lion** can attack a flock of sheep without being stopped, so the survivors of Israel will be stronger than the peoples among whom they live. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[as strong as a lion among the animals of the forest, as unstoppable as a young lion among the flocks of sheep]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 5:8
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 5:8 (#4) (id: en_tn_MIC_i970)

וְ⁠רָמַ֥ס וְ⁠טָרַ֖ף

**"both tramples and tears"**

The terms **tramples** and **tears** mean similar things. Micah is using the two terms together for emphasis. If it would be clearer for your readers, you could express the emphasis with a single phrase. Alternate translation: \[tears to pieces]

See: Doublet

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 5:8
* **Associated Articles:** Doublet (ID: `33266@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 5:9 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_ug9n)

תָּרֹ֥ם יָדְ⁠ךָ֖ עַל־צָרֶ֑י⁠ךָ וְ⁠כָל־אֹיְבֶ֖י⁠ךָ יִכָּרֵֽתוּ

**"May your hand rise over your adversaries, and may all of your enemies be cut off"**

Some ancient manuscripts read according to the ULT. Other ancient manuscripts read “Your hand will rise over your adversaries, and all of your enemies will be cut off.” In that case, “your” could refer either to Yahweh or to Jacob, who represents his descendants. If a translation of the Bible exists in your region, you may wish to use the reading that it uses. If a translation of the Bible does not exist in your region, you may wish to use the reading of the ULT.

See: Textual Variants

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 5:9
* **Associated Articles:** Textual Variants (ID: `33405@UWTranslationManual`)


## Micah 5:9 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_te1u)

תָּרֹ֥ם יָדְ⁠ךָ֖ עַל־צָרֶ֑י⁠ךָ וְ⁠כָל־אֹיְבֶ֖י⁠ךָ יִכָּרֵֽתוּ

**"May your hand rise over your adversaries, and may all of your enemies be cut off"**

Micah is saying what he wants Yahweh to do. See the discussion in the introduction to this book about “let” and “may” and follow the decision of your team of how a speaker will express in this context what he wants someone to do. Alternate translation: \[Yahweh, I desire that your hand rise over your adversaries and that all your enemies be destroyed]

See: Third\-Person Imperatives

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 5:9


## Micah 5:9 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_eyv9)

תָּרֹ֥ם יָדְ⁠ךָ֖ עַל־צָרֶ֑י⁠ךָ

**"May your hand rise over your adversaries"**

Here, **your hand** represents Yahweh’s power. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[May you triumph by your power over your adversaries]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 5:9
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationManual`)


## Micah 5:9 (#4) (id: en_tn_MIC_qwnt)

וְ⁠כָל־אֹיְבֶ֖י⁠ךָ יִכָּרֵֽתוּ

**"and may all of your enemies be cut off"**

The expression **be cut off** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[and may you cut off all of your enemies]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 5:9
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationManual`)


## Micah 5:9 (#5) (id: en_tn_MIC_ecbi)

וְ⁠כָל־אֹיְבֶ֖י⁠ךָ יִכָּרֵֽתוּ

**"and may all of your enemies be cut off"**

Micah is using **be cut off** as a common expression of the culture to mean “be destroyed.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and may all of your enemies be destroyed]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 5:9
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah - Chapter 6 Introduction (id: en_tn_MIC_k235)

Micah 6 Chapter Introduction
============================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

This chapter is written as a court case or lawsuit by Yahweh against His people. Yahweh calls his people to repentance and faithful living.

1. Yahweh brings his case against Israel (1–5\)
2. Yahweh desires justice, mercy, and humble obedience (6–8\)
3. Yahweh announces judgment for Israel’s sin (9–16\)

Translation Issues in This Chapter
----------------------------------

### Names in lawsuit

Moses, Aaron and Miriam are mentioned as witnesses for Yahweh and against Israel.

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:0


## Micah 6:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_h08b)

ק֚וּם רִ֣יב אֶת־הֶ⁠הָרִ֔ים וְ⁠תִשְׁמַ֥עְנָה הַ⁠גְּבָע֖וֹת קוֹלֶֽ⁠ךָ

**"Arise, contend before the mountains, and let the hills hear your voice"**

If it would be clearer in your language, you could translate this so that there is not a quotation within a quotation. Alternate translation: \[Yahweh commands me to arise and contend before the mountains, and to let the hills hear my voice]

See: Quotes within Quotes

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:1
* **Associated Articles:** Quotes within Quotes (ID: `33307@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

**"and let the hills hear"**

Yahweh is saying what he wants the hills to do. See the discussion in the introduction to this book about “let” and “may” and follow the decision of your team of how a speaker will express in this context what he wants to happen. Alternate translation: \[the hills should be able to hear]

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:1

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Micah 6:2 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_leo0)

שִׁמְע֤וּ הָרִים֙ אֶת־רִ֣יב יְהוָ֔ה וְ⁠הָ⁠אֵתָנִ֖ים מֹ֣סְדֵי אָ֑רֶץ

**"Hear the dispute of Yahweh, mountains, and permanent foundations of the earth"**

Micah is speaking to the **mountains** and the **foundations of the earth**, which he knows cannot hear him. He is doing this to communicate to the people listening to him that he feels very strongly about everything in all creation hearing the case that Yahweh is bringing against them. If it would be helpful in your language, you could translate these words as if he were speaking directly to the people about the mountains and the foundations of the earth. Alternate translation: \[Let all creation hear the dispute of Yahweh]

See: Apostrophe

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:2
* **Associated Articles:** Apostrophe (ID: `33259@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Israel (ID: `person:Israel`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Foundation (ID: `realia:Foundation`)

## Micah 6:2 (#4) (id: en_tn_MIC_n5i6)

כִּ֣י רִ֤יב לַֽ⁠יהוָה֙ עִם־עַמּ֔⁠וֹ וְ⁠עִם־יִשְׂרָאֵ֖ל יִתְוַכָּֽח

**"for a dispute{is} to Yahweh with his people, and with Israel he will argue"**

These two clauses mean basically the same thing. The second emphasizes the meaning of the first by repeating the same idea with different words. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could combine them and express the emphasis in another way. Alternate translation: \[for Yahweh has a dispute that he is bringing against his people, Israel]

See: Parallelism

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:2
* **Associated Articles:** Parallelism (ID: `33299@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 6:3 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_b8dd)

עַמִּ֛⁠י מֶה־עָשִׂ֥יתִי לְ⁠ךָ֖

**"My people, what have I done to you"**

This verse begins a direct quotation from Yahweh that continues through verse 5\. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could mark that this is a direct quote of Yahweh as modeled in the UST.

## Micah 6:3 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_zrcc)

מֶה־עָשִׂ֥יתִי לְ⁠ךָ֖ וּ⁠מָ֣ה הֶלְאֵתִ֑י⁠ךָ

**"what have I done to you, and how have I wearied you"**

Yahweh is using the question form to assert that he has done nothing to harm his people. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[I have done nothing to harm you or to burden you!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:3
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationManual`)


## Micah 6:3 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_nwwg)

עֲנֵ֥ה בִּֽ⁠י

**"Testify against me"**

By **Testify against me**, Yahweh is using the language of a court and saying that if they actually have anything to accuse him of, they should testify. The implication from the previous questions is that they have nothing to accuse him of. You could indicate this explicitly in your translation if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[Only if I have done anything wrong can you testify against me]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:3
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 6:5 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_rdp7)

זְכָר \& מַה־יָּעַ֗ץ בָּלָק֙ מֶ֣לֶךְ מוֹאָ֔ב וּ⁠מֶה־עָנָ֥ה אֹת֖⁠וֹ בִּלְעָ֣ם בֶּן־בְּע֑וֹר

**"remember what Balak, king of Moab, counseled, and what Balaam, son of Beor, answered him"**

Yahweh assumes that readers will know that he is referring to the events describe in Numbers 22–24\. The phrase **what Balak, king of Moab, counseled** refers to his plot to destroy Israel by hiring Balaam to curse them and **what Balaam, son of Beor, answered him** refers to the fact that Balaam told the king he could only bless the people rather than curse them. You could include this information in a footnote if that would be helpful to your readers.

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:5
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Balak (ID: `person:Balak`); Beor (ID: `person:Beor.2`); Moab (ID: `place:Moab`); Balaam (ID: `person:Balaam`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Shittim (ID: `place:Shittim`); Gilgal (ID: `place:Gilgal.2`)

## Micah 6:5 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_d761)

מִן־הַ⁠שִּׁטִּים֙ עַד־הַ⁠גִּלְגָּ֔ל

**"from Shittim to Gilgal"**

The implication is that Yahweh wants his people to remember what happened to their ancestors when they were able to miraculously cross the Jordan River (see Joshua 3 and 4\). **Shittim** was the location of their last camp east of the Jordan River, and **Gilgal**, was where first camped in the land that he had promised them. You could include this information if that would be helpful to your readers. Alternate translation: \[and remember what I did for your ancestors when they crossed the Jordan River]

See: Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:5
* **Associated Articles:** Assumed Knowledge and Implicit Information (ID: `33273@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Balak (ID: `person:Balak`); Beor (ID: `person:Beor.2`); Moab (ID: `place:Moab`); Balaam (ID: `person:Balaam`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Shittim (ID: `place:Shittim`); Gilgal (ID: `place:Gilgal.2`)

## Micah 6:6 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_vjbi)

בַּ⁠מָּה֙ אֲקַדֵּ֣ם יְהוָ֔ה

**"With what shall I come before Yahweh"**

This verse begins a shift from Yahweh’s words to the direct quotation spoken by a worshiper who represents the people. In your translation, use a natural way of indicating this shift in speaker.

See: Quotations and Quote Margins

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:6
* **Associated Articles:** Quotations and Quote Margins (ID: `33426@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 6:6 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_nriu)

אִכַּ֖ף לֵ⁠אלֹהֵ֣י

**"bow down to the God of"**

To **bow down** was a symbolic action by which people showed reverence and worship. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could explain the significance of this action in the text of your translation or in a footnote. Alternate translation: \[worship the God of]

See: Symbolic Action

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:6
* **Associated Articles:** Symbolic Action (ID: `33402@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 6:6 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_sge5)

אִכַּ֖ף

**"bow down"**

The speaker is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[With what shall I bow down]

* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 6:6 (#4) (id: en_tn_MIC_yksm)

לֵ⁠אלֹהֵ֣י מָר֑וֹם

**"to the God of the height"**

The speaker is using the possessive form to describe a **God** that is characterized by **the height**, that is, by his exalted position. If a speaker of your language would not use the possessive form for this, you could express this idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[to the exalted God]

See: Possession

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:6
* **Associated Articles:** Possession (ID: `33303@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Offering (ID: `keyterm:Offering`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Molten Image (ID: `realia:MoltenImage`); Cattle (ID: `fauna:Bull`)

## Micah 6:6 (#5) (id: en_tn_MIC_csj8)

בַּ⁠עֲגָלִ֖ים בְּנֵ֥י שָׁנָֽה

**"with calves, sons of a year"**

The speaker is using **calves, sons of a year** as a common expression of the culture to mean “calves that are one year old.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[with calves that are one year old]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:6
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 6:7 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_nwn4)

בְּ⁠אַלְפֵ֣י אֵילִ֔ים בְּ⁠רִֽבְב֖וֹת נַֽחֲלֵי־שָׁ֑מֶן

**"with thousands of rams, with ten thousands of rivers of oil"**

The speaker says **thousands of rams, with ten thousands of rivers of oil** as an extreme statement for emphasis. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use a different way to express the emphasis. Alternate translation: \[an enormous number of rams, with an unimaginable amount of oil]

## Micah 6:8 (#7) (id: en_tn_MIC_j0g9)

וְ⁠הַצְנֵ֥עַ לֶ֖כֶת עִם־אֱלֹהֶֽי⁠ךָ

**"and to be humble to walk with your God"**

Here Micah is speaking of how one lives as if it were **to walk** down a path and by **with your God**, he means that one should live one’s whole life in fellowship with God. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and to be humble as you live your whole life in fellowship with your God]

אֱלֹהֶֽי⁠ךָ

**"your God"**

Micah is using the possessive form **your God** to mean that Yahweh is the God whom the people worship, not that they possess or control him. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[Yahweh, the God whom you worship]

## Micah 6:9 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_omfk)

וְ⁠תוּשִׁיָּ֖ה יִרְאֶ֣ה שְׁמֶ֑⁠ךָ

**"and sound wisdom fears your name"**

Micah has been speaking to the people about Yahweh, but here he briefly turns aside and addresses Yahweh directly. He does this in order to express more powerfully his own reverence for Yahweh. If it would be helpful in your language, you could introduce this more clearly and put the rest of this address in quote marks. Alternate translation: \[and I say to Yahweh, “Sound wisdom fears your name”]

See: Aside

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:9
* **Associated Articles:** Aside (ID: `33260@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 6:9 (#7) (id: en_tn_MIC_sgvh)

וּ⁠מִ֥י יְעָדָֽ⁠הּ

**"and whoever he appointed"**

The phrase **and whoever he appointed** is a translation of the standard written Hebrew text as we have it today. Scholars have proposed, however, that the first word of the next verse should actually belong to this verse, and that the letters of the words have been divided incorrectly in the present text. By adding the first word of verse 10 to this phrase, changing two letters in it, and dividing the words differently, the meaning would be as in this alternate translation. You may want to follow the ULT or you may want to follow the major translation in your area. Alternate translation: \[and the assembly of the city]

See: Alternative Readings

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:9

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Sound Wisdom (ID: `keyterm:SoundWisdom`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Micah 6:10 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_p52s)

ע֗וֹד הַ⁠אִשׁ֙ בֵּ֣ית רָשָׁ֔ע אֹצְר֖וֹת רֶ֑שַׁע וְ⁠אֵיפַ֥ת רָז֖וֹן זְעוּמָֽה

**"Are there still {in} the house of the wicked treasuries of wickedness, and the ephah of leanness {that is} cursed"**

Yahweh is using the question form to accuse the people of the city of cheating others in their business dealings. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[There are still in the house of the wicked treasuries of wickedness, and the ephah of leanness that is cursed!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:10
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** House (ID: `realia:House`); Ephah (ID: `realia:Ephah`)

## Micah 6:10 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_aedi)

ע֗וֹד הַ⁠אִשׁ֙

**"Are there still"**

The phrase **Are there still** is a translation of the standard written Hebrew text as we have it today. The wording in the text, however, is very unusual, and scholars have proposed that the first word should be read with the previous verse and that a letter has dropped off of the second word. Reading the text in this way results in the following alternate translation. You may want to follow the ULT or you may want to follow the major translation in your area. Alternate translation: \[Can I forget]

See: Alternative Readings

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:10

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** House (ID: `realia:House`); Ephah (ID: `realia:Ephah`)

רָשָׁ֔ע

**"the wicked"**

Yahweh is using the adjective **wicked** as a noun to mean a certain kind of person. Your language may use adjectives in the same way. If not, you can translate this adjective with an equivalent phrase. Alternate translation: \[the person who is wicked]

See: Nominal Adjectives

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:10
* **Associated Articles:** Nominal Adjectives (ID: `33294@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** House (ID: `realia:House`); Ephah (ID: `realia:Ephah`)

רֶ֑שַׁע

**"wickedness"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **wickedness**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[things gained wickedly]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:10
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** House (ID: `realia:House`); Ephah (ID: `realia:Ephah`)

## Micah 6:10 (#5) (id: en_tn_MIC_zdyo)

וְ⁠אֵיפַ֥ת רָז֖וֹן

**"and the ephah of leanness"**

This **ephah** was equivalent to 22 liters or nearly two thirds of a bushel. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could express this in terms of modern measurements, either in the text or in a footnote.

See: Biblical Volume

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:10
* **Associated Articles:** Biblical Volume (ID: `33373@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** House (ID: `realia:House`); Ephah (ID: `realia:Ephah`)

## Micah 6:10 (#6) (id: en_tn_MIC_xwqu)

וְ⁠אֵיפַ֥ת רָז֖וֹן

**"and the ephah of leanness"**

Yahweh is using the possessive form to describe an **ephah** that is characterized by **leanness**, that is, a measure that holds less than it should. If a speaker of your language would not use the possessive form for this, you could express this idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[and the undersized ephah]

See: Possession

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:10
* **Associated Articles:** Possession (ID: `33303@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** House (ID: `realia:House`); Ephah (ID: `realia:Ephah`)

## Micah 6:10 (#7) (id: en_tn_MIC_mga6)

זְעוּמָֽה

**"that is} cursed"**

The expression **is cursed** is a passive verbal form. If it would be unnatural to use that form, you could express the idea in active form or in another way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[that I, Yahweh, have cursed]

See: Active or Passive

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:10
* **Associated Articles:** Active or Passive (ID: `33258@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** House (ID: `realia:House`); Ephah (ID: `realia:Ephah`)

## Micah 6:11 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_cc55)

הַ⁠אֶזְכֶּ֖ה בְּ⁠מֹ֣אזְנֵי רֶ֑שַׁע וּ⁠בְ⁠כִ֖יס אַבְנֵ֥י מִרְמָֽה

**"Would I be innocent with scales of wickedness, and with a bag of stones of deceit"**

Yahweh is using the question form to declare that no one who uses dishonest weights and measures can be innocent. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[I certainly would not be innocent if I used scales of wickedness and a bag of stones of deceit!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:11
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Balance (ID: `realia:Balance`); Deceit (ID: `keyterm:Deceit`); Bag (ID: `realia:Bag`)

## Micah 6:11 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_gmdp)

הַ⁠אֶזְכֶּ֖ה

**"Would I be innocent"**

The phrase **Would I be innocent** is a translation of the standard written Hebrew text as we have it today. It would also be possible to read the word as “Would I acquit.” You may want to follow the ULT or you may want to follow the major translation in your area. Alternate translation: \[Would I acquit someone]

See: Alternative Readings

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:11

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Balance (ID: `realia:Balance`); Deceit (ID: `keyterm:Deceit`); Bag (ID: `realia:Bag`)

## Micah 6:11 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_edak)

בְּ⁠מֹ֣אזְנֵי רֶ֑שַׁע

**"with scales of wickedness"**

Yahweh is using the possessive form to describe **scales** that are used for **wickedness** or cheating people. If a speaker of your language would not use the possessive form for this, you could express this idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[with unjust scales]

See: Possession

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:11
* **Associated Articles:** Possession (ID: `33303@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Balance (ID: `realia:Balance`); Deceit (ID: `keyterm:Deceit`); Bag (ID: `realia:Bag`)

## Micah 6:11 (#4) (id: en_tn_MIC_nitk)

אַבְנֵ֥י מִרְמָֽה

**"stones of deceit"**

Yahweh is using the possessive form to describe **stones**, which were used to weigh things they were selling, that are characterized by **deceit** because they were the wrong weight. If a speaker of your language would not use the possessive form for this, you could express this idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[deceitful stones]

See: Possession

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:11
* **Associated Articles:** Possession (ID: `33303@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Balance (ID: `realia:Balance`); Deceit (ID: `keyterm:Deceit`); Bag (ID: `realia:Bag`)

אֲשֶׁ֤ר עֲשִׁירֶ֨י⁠הָ֙

**"which her rich"**

The words **which** and **her** refer to the city that Yahweh mentioned in verse 9\. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[—the city whose rich]

## Micah 6:12 (#4) (id: en_tn_MIC_zpkr)

וְ⁠יֹשְׁבֶ֖י⁠הָ דִּבְּרוּ־שָׁ֑קֶר וּ⁠לְשׁוֹנָ֖⁠ם רְמִיָּ֥ה בְּ⁠פִי⁠הֶֽם

**"and her inhabitants speak falsehood, and their tongue {is} deceit in their mouth"**

These two clauses mean basically the same thing. The second emphasizes the meaning of the first by repeating the same idea with different words. You could use the approach which is modeled in the UST of connecting the phrases with a word other than “and” that shows that the second phrase is repeating the first one, not saying something additional. Another approach would be to combine them and express the emphasis in another way. Alternate translation: \[and all her inhabitants speak deceitfully]

See: Parallelism

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:12
* **Associated Articles:** Parallelism (ID: `33299@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Deceit (ID: `keyterm:Deceit`)

## Micah 6:12 (#5) (id: en_tn_MIC_ya3x)

שָׁ֑קֶר

**"falsehood"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **falsehood**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[her inhabitants speak falsely]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:12
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 6:12 (#6) (id: en_tn_MIC_f3tm)

**"and their tongue {is} deceit in their mouth"**

Here, **tongue** represents speech. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[and what they say is deceitful]

* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 6:13 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_u6ox)

וְ⁠גַם־אֲנִ֖י הֶחֱלֵ֣יתִי

**"And even I, I will make sick"**

Yahweh is stating the pronoun **I** separately, even though the verb translated as **I will make** already includes this meaning. He is doing that to emphasize that Yahweh himself is the one who will act. If a speaker of your language would use an explicit pronoun for the same purpose, you may want to use that construction in your translation. If not, your language may have other ways of showing the meaning here. Alternate translation: \[And even I, myself will make sick]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:13
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`)

## Micah 6:13 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_c7py)

הֶחֱלֵ֣יתִי הַכּוֹתֶ֑⁠ךָ

**"I will make sick the striking of you"**

Yahweh is using **make sick the striking of you** as a common expression of the culture to mean that he will administer an incurable injury on them. If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[I will strike you with an incurable wound]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:13
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`)

## Micah 6:13 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_jq4w)

הַכּוֹתֶ֑⁠ךָ

**"the striking of you"**

The pronouns **you** and **your** from here to the end of the chapter refer to the people of the city of Jerusalem whom Yahweh is now addressing directly after speaking about them in the previous verses. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[the striking of you, inhabitants of Jerusalem]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:13
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 6:14 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_k77n)

אַתָּ֤ה תֹאכַל֙

**"You, you will eat"**

Yahweh is stating the pronoun **you** separately, even though the verb translated as **you will eat** already includes this meaning. He is doing that to emphasize the contrast between the heavenly Judge and the sentence they will suffer. If a speaker of your language would use an explicit pronoun for the same purpose, you may want to use that construction in your translation. If not, your language may have other ways of showing the meaning here. Alternate translation: \[For your part, you will eat]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:14
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationManual`)


## Micah 6:14 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_vii4)

וְ⁠יֶשְׁחֲ⁠ךָ֖ בְּ⁠קִרְבֶּ֑⁠ךָ

**"for dysentery {will be} inside you"**

The word **dysentery** refers to a kind of disease of the bowels that empties the body of the food it takes in. If your readers would not be familiar with what dysentery is, you could use the name of a similar sickness that your readers would recognize, or you could use a more general term. Alternate translation: \[because your stomach will be empty]

See: Translate Unknowns

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:14
* **Associated Articles:** Translate Unknowns (ID: `33408@UWTranslationManual`)


## Micah 6:14 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_crqg)

וְ⁠תַסֵּג֙ וְ⁠לֹ֣א תַפְלִ֔יט וַ⁠אֲשֶׁ֥ר תְּפַלֵּ֖ט

**"And you will bring to safety but not deliver, and what you do deliver"**

The word translated as **bring to safety** could also be the word for “reach.” This leads some scholars to interpret these sentences in the context of birthing rather than warfare. You may want to follow the major translation in your area. Alternate translation: \[And you will reach birth, but not deliver the baby]

See: Alternative Readings

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:14

לַ⁠חֶ֥רֶב אֶתֵּֽן

**"I will give to the sword"**

Here, **the sword** represents warfare and being killed in battle. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[I will hand over to be killed in war]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:14
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationManual`)


## Micah 6:15 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_gxd2)

אַתָּ֥ה תִזְרַ֖ע וְ⁠לֹ֣א תִקְצ֑וֹר אַתָּ֤ה תִדְרֹֽךְ

**"You, you will sow but not reap; you, you will tread"**

Yahweh is stating the pronoun **you** separately, even though the verbs translated as **you will sow** and **you will tread** already include this meaning. If not, your language may have other ways of showing the meaning here. Alternate translation: \[You people of the city will certainly sow, but not reap, and you will certainly tread]

## Micah 6:16 (#6) (id: en_tn_MIC_g090)

וַ⁠תֵּלְכ֖וּ בְּ⁠מֹֽעֲצוֹתָ֑⁠ם

**"and you have walked in their counsels"**

Here Yahweh is speaking as if living according to the advice of Omri and Ahab were to have **walked in their counsels**. He is referring to the corrupt policies, economic injustice, and foreign idol worship (like Baal worship) institutionalized by Omri and his son, Ahab (see 1 Kings 16\). Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and you have followed their advice]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 6:16
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah - Chapter 7 Introduction (id: en_tn_MIC_kf61)

Micah 7 Chapter Introduction
============================

Structure and Formatting
------------------------

Yahweh hears the repentant and faithfully restores his people

1. Micah laments Israel’s corruption (1–6\)
2. Micah waits in hope for Yahweh’s salvation (7–13\)
3. Yahweh shows mercy and remains faithful to his covenant (14–20\)

Religious and Cultural Concepts in This Chapter
-----------------------------------------------

### Last days

This chapter looks forward to the hope of the coming savior for the faithful remnant. This is the future restoration of Israel when true peace will come.

### Prophet

The prophet was able to speak to God on behalf of the people. Often in this chapter, the prophet speaks in Israel’s place and offers repentance to Yahweh.

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:0


## Micah 7:1 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_te6o)

אַ֣לְלַי לִ֗⁠י

**"Woe to me"**

Micah is using a phrase that expresses a strong feeling of distress. There may be an equivalent word or phrase in your language that you can use in your translation to convey this feeling. Alternate translation: \[How sorrowful I feel]

See: Exclamations

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:1
* **Associated Articles:** Exclamations (ID: `33270@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`); Fig (ID: `flora:Fig`)

## Micah 7:1 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_p666)

כְּ⁠אָסְפֵּי־קַ֔יִץ כְּ⁠עֹלְלֹ֖ת בָּצִ֑יר

**"like the gatherings of summer fruit, like the gleanings of the grape harvest"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **the gatherings of summer fruit** and **the gleanings of the grape harvest** are what remains after everything worth having has already been taken, so is the land in which Micah is searching for a faithful person. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[like one searching where the summer fruit has already been gathered, like one searching where the grapes have already been harvested]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:1
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Vine (ID: `flora:Vine`); Fig (ID: `flora:Fig`)

## Micah 7:2 (#6) (id: en_tn_MIC_rd4j)

אִ֥ישׁ אֶת־אָחִ֖י⁠הוּ יָצ֥וּדוּ חֵֽרֶם

**"each man hunts his brother with a net"**

## Micah 7:2 (#7) (id: en_tn_MIC_lgt2)

אֶת־אָחִ֖י⁠הוּ

**"his brother"**

Micah is speaking as if a man’s **brother**, or fellow Israelite, were his prey, whom he hunts to harm rather than someone he should love and protect. If it would be helpful in your language, you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[each man tries to harm his fellow Israelite]

## Micah 7:3 (#5) (id: en_tn_MIC_bhdp)

וְ⁠הַ⁠גָּד֗וֹל דֹּבֵ֨ר הַוַּ֥ת נַפְשׁ֛⁠וֹ ה֖וּא

**"and the great one is speaking the desire of his very soul"**

Micah is not referring to a specific **great one**. He means people in positions of power in general. If it would be helpful to your readers, you could use an expression that would be more natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[and important people are speaking the desire of their very souls]

See: Generic Noun Phrases

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:3
* **Associated Articles:** Generic Noun Phrases (ID: `33277@UWTranslationManual`)

הַוַּ֥ת נַפְשׁ֛⁠וֹ ה֖וּא

**"the desire of his very soul"**

Here, **his very soul** represents the great one himself. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[of what he himself strongly desires]

**"and they weave it together"**

## Micah 7:4 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_absw)

כְּ⁠חֵ֔דֶק

**"is} like a brier"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **a brier** wounds anyone who touches it, so is even the best person among these people. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[injures anyone who comes near, just as a brier does]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:4
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 7:4 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_f4om)

יָשָׁ֖ר מִ⁠מְּסוּכָ֑ה

**"the upright, {worse} than a thorn hedge"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **a thorn hedge** injures those who come near it, so is the one who seems to be one of **the upright** people. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[the upright person injures people even more than a thorn hedge injures those who come near it]

**"guard the doors of your mouth"**

Micah is using **guard the doors of your mouth** as a common expression of the culture to mean “be very careful about what you say.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. **"a daughter\-in\-law against her mother\-in\-law"**

Micah is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[a daughter\-in\-law is rising up against her mother\-in\-law]

## Micah 7:6 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_z5lc)

אֹיְבֵ֥י אִ֖ישׁ

**"The enemies of a man"**

Micah is using the possessive form to describe **enemies** who are characterized by their relationship to **a man**. If a speaker of your language would not use the possessive form for this, you could express this idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[The ones who oppose a man]

See: Possession

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:6
* **Associated Articles:** Possession (ID: `33303@UWTranslationManual`)


## Micah 7:6 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_z7n7)

אַנְשֵׁ֥י בֵיתֽ⁠וֹ

**"are} the men of his house"**

Micah is using the possessive form to describe **the men** who are characterized by living in **his house**. If a speaker of your language would not use the possessive form for this, you could express this idea in another way as modeled in the UST.

See: Possession

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:6
* **Associated Articles:** Possession (ID: `33303@UWTranslationManual`)


## Micah 7:7 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_iz9i)

וַ⁠אֲנִי֙ בַּ⁠יהוָ֣ה אֲצַפֶּ֔ה

**"But I, I will watch for Yahweh"**

Micah is stating the pronoun **I** separately, even though the verb translated as **I will watch** already includes this meaning. He is doing that to emphasize the contrast between himself and the people he has been describing. If a speaker of your language would use an explicit pronoun for the same purpose, you may want to use that construction in your translation. If not, your language may have other ways of showing the meaning here. Alternate translation: \[But unlike these people, I will watch for Yahweh]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:7
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Salvation (ID: `keyterm:Salvation`)

לֵ⁠אלֹהֵ֣י יִשְׁעִ֑⁠י

**"the God of my salvation"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **salvation**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[the God who saves me]

* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 7:7 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_ktax)

אֱלֹהָֽ⁠י

**"My God"**

Moses is not using this possessive form **My God** to indicate that he possesses or controls Yahweh. Rather Moses is using these words to indicate that Yahweh is the God whom he worships. Alternate translation: \[Yahweh, the God whom I worship,]

## Micah 7:8 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_jrtk)

אַֽל־תִּשְׂמְחִ֤י אֹיַ֨בְתִּ⁠י֙ לִ֔⁠י כִּ֥י נָפַ֖לְתִּי קָ֑מְתִּי כִּֽי־אֵשֵׁ֣ב בַּ⁠חֹ֔שֶׁךְ יְהוָ֖ה א֥וֹר לִֽ⁠י

**"Do not rejoice over me, my enemy! Though I have fallen; I will arise. Though I sit in the darkness; Yahweh {is} a light to me"**

In verses 8 through 10 Micah speaks on behalf of his people. If it would be helpful, you could translate the singular pronouns as plurals, as in the UST.

See: Synecdoche

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:8
* **Associated Articles:** Synecdoche (ID: `33315@UWTranslationManual`)

אֹיַ֨בְתִּ⁠י֙

**"my enemy"**

By **my enemy**, Micah refers to the nation that Micah foresees will conquer Israel. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[you enemy nation of my people]

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:8
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 7:8 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_qcul)

נָפַ֖לְתִּי קָ֑מְתִּי

**"I have fallen; I will arise"**

Here Micah is speaking on behalf of the people about suffering defeat as if it were to have **fallen** down and of recovering as if it were to **arise** again. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[we have suffered disaster; we will be restored]

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:8
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 7:8 (#4) (id: en_tn_MIC_wvz3)

אֵשֵׁ֣ב בַּ⁠חֹ֔שֶׁךְ יְהוָ֖ה א֥וֹר לִֽ⁠י

**"I sit in the darkness; Yahweh {is} a light to me"**

Here Micah is speaking of the trouble his people are experiencing as if it were **the darkness** and of Yahweh’s help as if it were **a light**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[we are in trouble; Yahweh helps us]

* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 7:9 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_bzx9)

זַ֤עַף יְהוָה֙ אֶשָּׂ֔א כִּ֥י חָטָ֖אתִי ל֑⁠וֹ

**"I will bear the rage of Yahweh, for I have sinned against him"**

If it would be more natural in your language, you could reverse the order of these phrases, since the second phrase gives the reason for the result that the first phrase describes. Alternate translation: \[Because I have sinned against Yahweh, I will bear his rage]

## Micah 7:10 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_f7a7)

**"Then may my enemy see, so that shame covers her"**

The pronoun **my** refers to Micah, who speaks here on behalf of his people, and **my enemy** and **her** refer to the nation that Micah foresees will conquer Israel. **"Then may my enemy see"**

Micah is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context of the end of the last verse if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[Then may my enemy also see what Yahweh has done]

* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationManual`)

וְ⁠תֵרֶ֤א אֹיַ֨בְתִּ⁠י֙

**"Then may my enemy see"**

Micah is saying what he wants his enemy to do. See the discussion in the introduction to this book about “let” and “may” and follow the decision of your team of how a speaker will express in this context what he wants someone to do. Alternate translation: \[I desire that my enemy see] or \[Please allow my enemy to see]

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:10
* **Associated Articles:** Imperatives — Other Uses (ID: `33284@UWTranslationManual`)

**"so that shame covers her"**

Here Micah is speaking as if **shame** were a garment that **covers** a person. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[so that she becomes utterly ashamed]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:10
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

וּ⁠תְכַסֶּ֣⁠הָ בוּשָׁ֔ה

**"so that shame covers her"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **shame**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[so that she feels ashamed]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:10
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Micah 7:10 (#6) (id: en_tn_MIC_cxsk)

אַיּ֖⁠וֹ יְהוָ֣ה אֱלֹהָ֑יִ⁠ךְ

**"Where {is} Yahweh your God"**

The enemy is using the question form to mock Micah by suggesting that Yahweh is unwilling or unable to help him. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[Yahweh your God is nowhere to be found!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:10
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Micah 7:10 (#7) (id: en_tn_MIC_jxau)

יְהוָ֣ה אֱלֹהָ֑יִ⁠ךְ

**"Yahweh your God"**

The enemy is not using this possessive form **your God** to indicate that Micah possesses or controls Yahweh but rather to emphasize that Yahweh is the God that he worships. Alternate translation: \[Yahweh, the God whom you worship]

See: Possession

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:10
* **Associated Articles:** Possession (ID: `33303@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Micah 7:10 (#8) (id: en_tn_MIC_ddf4)

עֵינַ⁠י֙ תִּרְאֶ֣ינָּה בָּ֔⁠הּ

**"My eyes will look at her"**

Micah is using one part of a person, his **eyes**, to mean all of him in the act of watching his enemy’s downfall. If it would be helpful in your language, you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[I will watch what happens to her]

See: Synecdoche

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:10
* **Associated Articles:** Synecdoche (ID: `33315@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Micah 7:10 (#9) (id: en_tn_MIC_se1x)

כְּ⁠טִ֥יט חוּצֽוֹת

**"like the mud of the streets"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **the mud of the streets** is trampled underfoot and despised, so will Micah’s enemy be. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[as thoroughly despised as the mud that people tread on in the streets]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:10
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`)

## Micah 7:11 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_acvh)

י֖וֹם לִ⁠בְנ֣וֹת גְּדֵרָ֑יִ⁠ךְ

**"A day to build your walls"**

Micah is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[A day will come to build your walls]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:11
* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Decree (ID: `keyterm:Decree`)

## Micah 7:11 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_c8pc)

גְּדֵרָ֑יִ⁠ךְ

**"your walls"**

The pronoun **your** refers to the people of Zion. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[your walls, people of Zion]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:11
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Decree (ID: `keyterm:Decree`)

## Micah 7:12 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_ud9a)

וְ⁠עָדֶ֣י⁠ךָ יָב֔וֹא

**"when as far as you he will come"**

The pronoun **you** refers to Zion, or Jerusalem, and the pronoun **he** refers to each person who will come to the city of Jerusalem from the distant places named in this verse. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[when many people will come to Jerusalem]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:12
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`); Assyria (ID: `place:Assyria`); Euphrates River (ID: `place:Euphrates`)

## Micah 7:13 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_h8s9)

וְ⁠הָיְתָ֥ה הָ⁠אָ֛רֶץ לִ⁠שְׁמָמָ֖ה

**"And the land will become a desolation"**

Micah uses the word **And** here to indicate a contrast between the restoration that he has just described and the desolation that will come upon the rest of the earth. In your translation, indicate this contrast in a way that is natural in your language. Alternate translation: \[But all the rest of the land will become a desolation]

See: Connect — Contrast Relationship

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:13
* **Associated Articles:** Connect — Contrast Relationship (ID: `33329@UWTranslationManual`)


## Micah 7:13 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_qcop)

לִ⁠שְׁמָמָ֖ה

**"a desolation"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **desolation**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[desolate]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:13
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationManual`)


## Micah 7:13 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_vwsr)

יֹֽשְׁבֶ֑י⁠הָ מִ⁠פְּרִ֖י מַֽעַלְלֵי⁠הֶֽם

**"her inhabitants, from the fruit of their deeds"**

The pronoun **her** refers to the land, and the pronoun **their** refers to the people who live in the land. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[the land’s inhabitants, from the fruit of those people’s deeds]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:13
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationManual`)


## Micah 7:13 (#4) (id: en_tn_MIC_tare)

מִ⁠פְּרִ֖י מַֽעַלְלֵי⁠הֶֽם

**"from the fruit of their deeds"**

Here Micah is speaking of the results of what people do as if they were **the fruit** that a plant produces. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[from the results of their deeds]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:13
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)


## Micah 7:14 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_d82m)

רְעֵ֧ה עַמְּ⁠ךָ֣ בְ⁠שִׁבְטֶ֗⁠ךָ צֹ֚אן נַֽחֲלָתֶ֔⁠ךָ

**"Shepherd your people with your rod, the flock of your inheritance"**

Here Micah is speaking as if Yahweh were a shepherd and as if Yahweh’s people were **the flock** that he tends. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[Care for your people as a shepherd cares for his flock, the people who belong to you]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:14
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Gilead (ID: `place:Gilead`); To Cause to Possess (ID: `keyterm:Possess`); Bashan (ID: `place:Bashan`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Sandalwood (ID: `flora:Sandalwood`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Rod (ID: `realia:Rod`); Staff (ID: `realia:Staff`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`)

## Micah 7:14 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_btj0)

רְעֵ֧ה עַמְּ⁠ךָ֣

**"Shepherd your people"**

The word translated as **Shepherd** is an imperative, but it communicates a polite request to a superior rather than a command. If the imperative does not communicate a request for your readers, then use a form in your language that communicates a polite request to a superior. Alternate translation: \[Yahweh, please shepherd your people]

See: Imperatives — Other Uses

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:14
* **Associated Articles:** Imperatives — Other Uses (ID: `33284@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Gilead (ID: `place:Gilead`); To Cause to Possess (ID: `keyterm:Possess`); Bashan (ID: `place:Bashan`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Sandalwood (ID: `flora:Sandalwood`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Rod (ID: `realia:Rod`); Staff (ID: `realia:Staff`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`)

## Micah 7:14 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_xfwo)

צֹ֚אן נַֽחֲלָתֶ֔⁠ךָ

**"the flock of your inheritance"**

Micah is using the possessive form to describe **the flock** that is characterized by being **your inheritance**, that is, the people whom Yahweh has chosen to belong to him. If a speaker of your language would not use the possessive form for this, you could express this idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[the flock that belongs to you]

See: Possession

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:14
* **Associated Articles:** Possession (ID: `33303@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Gilead (ID: `place:Gilead`); To Cause to Possess (ID: `keyterm:Possess`); Bashan (ID: `place:Bashan`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Sandalwood (ID: `flora:Sandalwood`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Rod (ID: `realia:Rod`); Staff (ID: `realia:Staff`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`)

## Micah 7:14 (#4) (id: en_tn_MIC_ivlw)

יִרְע֥וּ

**"May they graze"**

Micah is saying what he wants Yahweh to do. See the discussion in the introduction to this book about “let” and “may” and follow the decision of your team of how a speaker will express in this context what he wants someone to do. Alternate translation: \[I desire that they graze] or \[Please allow them to graze]

See: Third\-Person Imperatives

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:14

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Gilead (ID: `place:Gilead`); To Cause to Possess (ID: `keyterm:Possess`); Bashan (ID: `place:Bashan`); Flock (ID: `fauna:Flock`); Sandalwood (ID: `flora:Sandalwood`); Goat (ID: `fauna:Goat`); Rod (ID: `realia:Rod`); Staff (ID: `realia:Staff`); Sheep (ID: `fauna:Lamb`)

## Micah 7:15 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_w79b)

כִּ⁠ימֵ֥י צֵאתְ⁠ךָ֖ מֵ⁠אֶ֣רֶץ מִצְרָ֑יִם

**"As in the days of your going out from the land of Egypt"**

The point of this comparison is that just as Yahweh did wonderful things in **the days of your going out from the land of Egypt**, so he will do wonderful things for his people again. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[Just as I did wonderful things when you came out from the land of Egypt]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:15
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`)

## Micah 7:15 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_qi1k)

אַרְאֶ֖⁠נּוּ

**"I will make him see"**

The pronoun **I** refers to Yahweh, who begins to speak here, and the pronoun **him** refers to the people of Israel. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[I, Yahweh, will make my people Israel see]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:15
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`)

## Micah 7:15 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_myzp)

נִפְלָאֽוֹת

**"wonders"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **wonders**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[marvelous things that I do]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:15
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Egypt (ID: `place:Egypt`)

## Micah 7:16 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_bs76)

יִרְא֤וּ גוֹיִם֙

**"The nations will see"**

Micah is leaving out some of the words that in many languages a sentence would need in order to be complete. You can supply these words from the context of the previous verse if that would be clearer in your language. Alternate translation: \[The nations will see what Yahweh has done]

See: Ellipsis

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:16
* **Associated Articles:** Ellipsis (ID: `33267@UWTranslationManual`)


## Micah 7:16 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_loqo)

גְּבֽוּרָתָ֑⁠ם

**"their might"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **might**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[the mighty things they thought they had done]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:16
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationManual`)


## Micah 7:16 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_axbs)

יָשִׂ֤ימוּ יָד֙ עַל־פֶּ֔ה

**"They will put a hand over mouth"**

Micah is using **put a hand over mouth** as a common expression of the culture to mean “be too astonished to speak.” If this phrase does not have that meaning for your readers, you could use a comparable expression from your language that does have that meaning, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[They will be too astonished to speak]

See: Idiom

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:16
* **Associated Articles:** Idiom (ID: `33283@UWTranslationManual`)


## Micah 7:16 (#4) (id: en_tn_MIC_fjyc)

אָזְנֵי⁠הֶ֖ם תֶּחֱרַֽשְׁנָה

**"their ears will be deaf"**

Here, **their ears** represents their ability to hear. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[they will lose their ability to hear]

See: Metonymy

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:16
* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationManual`)


## Micah 7:17 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_iil3)

יְלַחֲכ֤וּ עָפָר֙ כַּ⁠נָּחָ֔שׁ

**"They will lick dust like the serpent"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **the serpent** crawls low along the ground in the dust, so will the nations be brought low before Yahweh. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[They will be brought low, licking the dust like the serpent that crawls along the ground]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:17
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Snake (ID: `fauna:Snake`)

## Micah 7:17 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_c95q)

יְלַחֲכ֤וּ

**"They will lick"**

The pronoun **They**, here and throughout this verse, refers to the nations mentioned in the previous verse. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[The nations will lick]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:17
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Snake (ID: `fauna:Snake`)

## Micah 7:17 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_iogd)

כְּ⁠זֹחֲלֵ֣י אֶ֔רֶץ

**"like crawling things of the earth"**

The point of this comparison is that just as **crawling things of the earth** creep along the ground, so will the nations come out of their strongholds in fear and humiliation. If it would be helpful in your language, you could make this point explicitly. Alternate translation: \[like creatures that creep along the ground in fear]

See: Simile

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:17
* **Associated Articles:** Simile (ID: `33314@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Snake (ID: `fauna:Snake`)

## Micah 7:17 (#4) (id: en_tn_MIC_c7t5)

אֱלֹהֵ֨י⁠נוּ֙

**"our God"**

Micah is using this possessive form **our God** to mean that Yahweh is the God whom he and his people worship, not that they possess or control him. If it would be helpful in your language, you could state this explicitly as modeled in the UST.

See: Possession

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:17
* **Associated Articles:** Possession (ID: `33303@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Snake (ID: `fauna:Snake`)

## Micah 7:17 (#5) (id: en_tn_MIC_hv2b)

מִמֶּֽ⁠ךָּ

**"because of you"**

The pronoun **you** could refer to: (1\) Yahweh, since his judgment makes sinners afraid, and the next verse addresses Yahweh directly, or (2\) Israel, since Israel is addressed directly in verse 15, with no indication that that has changed. Alternate translation: \[because of you, Israel]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:17
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Fear (ID: `keyterm:Fear`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Snake (ID: `fauna:Snake`)

## Micah 7:18 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_eugl)

מִי־אֵ֣ל כָּמ֗וֹ⁠ךָ נֹשֵׂ֤א עָוֺן֙ וְ⁠עֹבֵ֣ר עַל־פֶּ֔שַׁע לִ⁠שְׁאֵרִ֖ית נַחֲלָת֑⁠וֹ

**"Who is a God like you, bearing iniquity and passing over transgression for the remnant of his inheritance"**

Micah is using the question form to declare that no other god is like Yahweh. If a speaker of your language would not use the question form for that purpose, you could translate this as a statement or as an exclamation. Alternate translation: \[No other God is like you, Yahweh, bearing iniquity and passing over transgression for the remnant of his inheritance!]

See: Rhetorical Question

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:18
* **Associated Articles:** Rhetorical Question (ID: `33310@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); Forgive (ID: `keyterm:Forgive`); Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`); To Cause to Possess (ID: `keyterm:Possess`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Kindness (ID: `keyterm:Kindness`)

## Micah 7:18 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_ck3b)

נֹשֵׂ֤א עָוֺן֙ וְ⁠עֹבֵ֣ר עַל־פֶּ֔שַׁע

**"bearing iniquity and passing over transgression"**

The expressions **bearing iniquity** and **passing over transgression** mean similar things. Micah is using the two terms together for emphasis. If it would be clearer for your readers, you could express the emphasis with a single phrase. Alternate translation: \[fully forgiving all the sinful actions]

See: Doublet

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:18
* **Associated Articles:** Doublet (ID: `33266@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 7:18 (#3) (id: en_tn_MIC_gq63)

עָוֺן֙

**"iniquity"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **iniquity**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[the bad things people do]

See: Abstract Nouns

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:18
* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); Forgive (ID: `keyterm:Forgive`); Rebel (ID: `keyterm:Rebel`); To Cause to Possess (ID: `keyterm:Possess`); LORD (ID: `deity:Lord`); Kindness (ID: `keyterm:Kindness`)

## Micah 7:18 (#4) (id: en_tn_MIC_gn8t)

פֶּ֔שַׁע

**"transgression"**

If your language does not use an abstract noun for the idea of **transgression**, you could express the same idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[what people have done wrong]

* **Associated Articles:** Abstract Nouns (ID: `33257@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 7:18 (#5) (id: en_tn_MIC_wujn)

לִ⁠שְׁאֵרִ֖ית נַחֲלָת֑⁠וֹ

**"for the remnant of his inheritance"**

Micah is using the possessive form to describe **the remnant** that is characterized by being **his inheritance**, that is, the people who belong to Yahweh who have remained faithful to him. If a speaker of your language would not use the possessive form for this, you could express this idea in another way. Alternate translation: \[for those people who belong to him because they are still faithful to him]

See: Possession

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:18
* **Associated Articles:** Possession (ID: `33303@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 7:18 (#6) (id: en_tn_MIC_velc)

נַחֲלָת֑⁠וֹ

**"his inheritance"**

Even though Micah is addressing Yahweh directly, he is speaking of him in the third person using **his** and “he”. If it would be helpful in your language, you could translate these pronouns throughout this section in the second person for consistency as modeled in the UST.

See: First, Second or Third Person

* **Associated Articles:** First, Second or Third Person (ID: `33256@UWTranslationManual`)

**"He does not hold his nose forever"**

Here, **his nose** represents Yahweh’s anger. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[He does not hold on to his anger forever]

* **Associated Articles:** Metonymy (ID: `33293@UWTranslationManual`)

## Micah 7:19 (#1) (id: en_tn_MIC_izph)

יִכְבֹּ֖שׁ עֲוֺֽנֹתֵ֑י⁠נוּ

**"He will subdue our iniquities"**

Here Micah is speaking as if the sins of his people were enemies that Yahweh will **subdue**. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[He will overcome the power of our iniquities]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:19
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`)

## Micah 7:19 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_iq51)

עֲוֺֽנֹתֵ֑י⁠נוּ וְ⁠תַשְׁלִ֛יךְ בִּ⁠מְצֻל֥וֹת יָ֖ם כָּל־חַטֹּאותָֽ⁠ם

**"our iniquities, and you will throw into the depths of the sea all of their sins"**

The pronouns **our** and **their** both refer to Micah and his people. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[all of our people’s iniquities, and you will throw into the depths of the sea all of our sins]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:19
* **Associated Articles:** Pronouns — When to Use Them (ID: `33424@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`)

עֲוֺֽנֹתֵ֑י⁠נוּ

## Micah 7:19 (#4) (id: en_tn_MIC_ar5w)

וְ⁠תַשְׁלִ֛יךְ בִּ⁠מְצֻל֥וֹת יָ֖ם כָּל־חַטֹּאותָֽ⁠ם

**"and you will throw into the depths of the sea all of their sins"**

Here Micah is speaking as if the sins of his people were objects that Yahweh could **throw into the depths of the sea**, where no one could ever recover them. Your language may have a comparable expression that you can use in your translation, or you could state the meaning plainly. Alternate translation: \[and you will completely remove all of their sins forever]

See: Metaphor

* **Associated Passages:** Micah 7:19
* **Associated Articles:** Metaphor (ID: `33292@UWTranslationManual`)

* **Associated ACAI Concepts:** Passion (ID: `keyterm:Ardour`); To Sin (ID: `keyterm:Sin`)

## Micah 7:19 (#5) (id: en_tn_MIC_tcol)

וְ⁠תַשְׁלִ֛יךְ

**"and you will throw"**

The pronoun **you** refers to Yahweh, whom Micah addresses directly here after speaking about him in the previous sentence. It may be helpful to clarify this for your readers. Alternate translation: \[and you, Yahweh, will throw]

See: Pronouns — When to Use Them

## Micah 7:20 (#2) (id: en_tn_MIC_ha5s)

לְ⁠יַֽעֲקֹ֔ב חֶ֖סֶד לְ⁠אַבְרָהָ֑ם

**"to Jacob, covenant faithfulness to Abraham"**

Here, **Jacob** and **Abraham** represent the descendants of these two men. If it would be helpful in your language, you could use an equivalent expression or plain language. Alternate translation: \[to Jacob’s descendants, covenant faithfulness to those who descended from Abraham]

See: Metonymy
